Wait one tick, let the measure configuration be laid out, and read on the next. **And the measure configuration is `position: absolute` PLUS `width: max-content`, both of them**: absolute alone came back zero because Taffy does not shrink-to-fit an out-of-flow box the way CSS does, and `max-content` alone would be measured in flow, which is a frame of the whole row jumping apart and snapping back. The cost is two setup frames during which the element is invisible and takes no space |
| **A drop position is RAW SURFACE PIXELS; a float's insets are its OWNER's local space** | Two conversions apart, and both place the window neatly somewhere wrong — the raw position is out by `uiScale`, and the drop overlay's own local space starts below the caption and inside the rails. `containsScreenPoint` is the giveaway for which kind of coordinate a listener is holding, the same tell the drag-callback rule already records |
| **Content with its own top bar goes in a window's CAPTION, not above it** — `WindowChrome` + `WindowFrame.setContent`, and the bar is MOVED rather than copied or hidden | Two headers stacked on each other is what every desktop toolkit invented client-side decorations to fix (GTK's `HeaderBar`, VS Code's custom title bar, IntelliJ's New UI, WinUI's `ExtendsContentIntoTitleBar`). Moving matters more than it looks: a copy, or a "hide yours" flag, leaves two bars in the tree with every listener and every piece of state on whichever one is not on screen — and it looks correct. The return trip has to restore the **internal-child flag** too, since a workbench holds its menu bar as one and `remove` refuses those. The caption is `min-height`, never `height`, or it clips what it adopts |
| **A maximised window restores on the first MOVEMENT of a drag, never on the press** | Restoring on the press means the first press of a double-click restores and the second re-maximises, so double-clicking a maximised caption appears to do nothing at all — the two gestures share one listener. Windows agrees: click-and-hold on a maximised title bar does nothing there either, and it is the movement that tears the window loose |
| **A drag callback's coordinates are ALREADY CONVERTED; a mouse-DOWN listener's are RAW** — `Drag.tick` runs `screenToLocal` against the drag source before calling the listener | Converting again halves the number, so at the default `uiScale` of 2 everything lands at about half the distance — a restore-drag put the window back a third of the way along the caption instead of three quarters. The two spaces are a factor apart, which is exactly the size of error that looks like a bad constant rather than a wrong frame of reference. `containsScreenPoint` is the giveaway for which is which: it takes the raw one. **This row used to say "ALREADY LOCAL", and that one word cost a second bug in the opposite direction** — `screenToLocal` converts out of SURFACE pixels into the element's layout space and does **not** subtract the element's own origin, so its answer is an ABSOLUTE layout coordinate comparable to `getRuntimeCache().getX()`. That is what `isMouseOverElement` relies on (it compares its argument against `rectX`) and what `restoreUnderPointer` relies on (it subtracts the bar's X to get an offset along the caption). Read as "relative to the source", it invites adding the source's origin back — which is what `snapZoneAt` did, so the snap zone a drag reported was displaced by however far along the desktop the window happened to be and armed at roughly double speed. It presents as "snap triggers too early", never as a conversion, because it is wrong by a different amount every time |
| **A raise is a `z-index` assignment and NEVER a child-list move** — `Desktop.raise` writes IMPORTANT-origin z; `sortedChildren` already keeps paint order and hit-testing agreeing | `remove`/`append` is `unregisterElement`/`registerElement` over the whole frame subtree — session state captured and re-applied, modal/popover/close-watcher stacks popped, every Taffy node destroyed and rebuilt — and a raise happens **on a click**, which is exactly when a widget must never rebuild the elements it is being clicked on. It would work, and the window would slowly lose state nobody could attribute to clicking it |
| **A window's own edges belong to its eight resize handles** — anything pressing "just inside" a frame in a test or a fixture hits `__resizer-left__`, not the content | The handles are absolutely positioned over the border box and hit-test first, which is correct. It presents as an activation or focus bug: the press lands, focus walks up to the frame (a resizer is not focusable), and the control four pixels away never sees it. Press an element's CENTRE |
| **The desktop's window layer is the WORK AREA, and it only means anything once it is `.__windows__`** | Its whole geometry is one CSS rule; without the class the layer sizes to content, its children are all absolutely positioned, and it measures 0x0. Nothing then fails — every rule that reads the work area is guarded against a zero box on purpose, so the clamp and the cascade both quietly stand down and windows land wherever they were asked to go. Cost a full test run to see, and the fix was one `addClass` |
| **A freshly allocated FBO loses the first draw made into it — and warming ONE program does not vouch for another** | `CgUiPaintContext.warmUpLayerFbo` has documented this since the layer pool existed, with the symptom stated exactly: content simply missing on frame 1, permanently correct from frame 2. What its one transparent quad warms is the BLIT material, which is all a pooled layer ever needs. A window snapshot draws a whole window's worth of programs into its own target, and text is not one of them — `CgTextRenderer` owns its own renderer, instance buffer and material — so the first photograph of a window came back complete **except for the editor's text**, which reads as a race in the editor rather than in the surface it was drawn onto. Enumerating programs is the wrong shape of fix: draw the real content twice on a fresh target and discard the first pass |
| **`AnchoredPlacement.resolve` LEFT-ALIGNS on the cross axis; it does not centre** | Correct for what it was written for — a dropdown hangs from its button's left edge — and wrong for anything that is a label for the thing beneath it. A taskbar preview sits over the MIDDLE of its entry, as Windows' does, so it centres itself after resolving and re-clamps, because an entry near either end of a centred strip would otherwise push the panel off screen. Centre it in the consumer, never in `AnchoredPlacement`, which every menu in the engine depends on |
| **An `overlay` paints into its OWN element's box, so an icon on a fresh child of a sized slot shows nothing** | The sheet sizes the slot (`__pre-icon__`); a new child inside it has no size of its own, so it is a 0x0 box with an icon in it. Nothing appears and nothing in the tree says why — no error, no warning, a correct-looking element with a correct-looking overlay. `Taskbar.applyIcon` puts it on the slot itself and that is the pattern; a preview panel got it wrong in the obvious way |
| **A floating panel that animates ACROSS its own anchor steals the hover from it** | A taskbar preview rises from below its resting place and is promoted to the top layer, so for the first part of every entrance it covers the entry it belongs to and wins the hit test. The entry's `:hover` blinks off and back on as the panel passes, the panel's own hover rules churn with it, and — worse — the leave logic runs against a pointer that never went anywhere. Unhittable for the duration of any motion, restored when it settles: a control you cannot aim at yet is not a control |
| **A window animation animates TRANSFORM and OPACITY, never layout — and it is on the GESTURE, never on the API** | What every compositor does: DWM, Quartz and Mutter all animate a window's *surface*. `Transform` is layout-free here (Taffy never sees it), so a window flying into the taskbar reflows nothing and its contents are not re-measured sixty times a second; transitioning `left`/`top`/`width`/`height` animates the same picture and re-runs layout for the whole window to do it, which for a maximise — the moment the window is largest and holds the most — is the worst time to pay it. **A SIZE change is the exception and animates LAYOUT instead**, because it reflows the window's content: FLIP — jump layout to the destination, apply the inverse transform, ease it away — draws the *destination's* layout at the *source's* geometry, so restoring a maximised window reflowed its text for a 600px window and drew it 3x magnified, opening the animation on a frame of enormous text. GNOME solves this with a painted clone cross-faded against the live actor, and tellingly does so for `_sizeChangeWindow` and nothing else — its map, destroy and minimise need no clone for exactly the reason ours do not. Animating layout cannot be visually wrong and needs no snapshot machinery; the cost is a reflow per frame, which `UIResizer` already pays on every frame of a resize drag. And `hide()`/`show()`/`destroy()` stay **synchronous**: an OS animates what the user did, not a session restoring twelve windows or an eviction, and making the API itself deferred would put a frame between every caller and the thing it just asked for |
| **A COMPOSITOR animation must not run through the CASCADE — it is a timeline, and the cascade is for rest states** | The window animations were CSS `transition`s first, and the attempt is worth keeping because every one of its four failure modes was silent and none of them was the cascade doing its job badly. It is the wrong instrument: a compositor animation moves several properties that must change **together**, and the cascade resolves them independently. (1) `transition` is itself a property resolved in the same pass as the ones it governs, so lifting a suppression and changing a value in one frame could resolve the value first, see `transition: none` and apply it instantly — every time, since resolution order is stable, which read as the feature never having been wired up. (2) An `INLINE` cleanup value written to end one animation permanently outranks the stylesheet class used to start the next, so a window that had once been maximised could never animate closed again. (3) `transform-origin` is not interpolable and must be pinned for an animation's whole life; reset by the very write being transitioned, a maximise eased about the CENTRE having been computed about the CORNER — which is what "chopped and flickering" turned out to be. (4) Completion could only be discovered by polling. The answer is the shape every real compositor uses — `CABasicAnimation`, `ValueAnimator`, `AnimationController`: from, to, duration, curve, a per-frame tick, a completion callback. `WindowAnimation` is that, over the engine's own `ActiveTransition` maths, writing at **ANIMATION origin** — which sits above `IMPORTANT` and which the cascade's diff deliberately ignores, so the writes can neither start a transition nor fight a sheet, and ending them reveals the resting value with no cleanup value to get wrong |
| **A WINDOW IS DRAWN WHETHER OR NOT IT HAS BEEN PLACED, and the first window on an empty desktop cannot be placed on the pass that adds it** | `placeByCascade` needs the work area AND the frame measured, and neither is: the compositor deliberately takes up no space until a window exists, so the layer is 0x0 at the moment the frame's own `onLayoutChanged` asks. It returns without placing and without marking the frame placed — correct, and it leaves nobody to try again, because growing the work area does not resize a frame that has a width and a height of its own, so that callback never fires a second time. The window is therefore painted at its unplaced position, hard against the left of the work area, until something unrelated dirties layout. Silent, and it presents as an ANIMATION fault: during an entry animation the window appears to fly in from the left of the SCREEN — which is the tell, since a wrong `transform-origin` can only ever shrink a window toward its own edge, never bring it in from the application's. Only on a window's FIRST open, because `placed` is false exactly once. The work area's own layout callback places what is unplaced and re-clamps what is not, which is the one moment placement becomes possible |
| **AN ANIMATION'S CLOCK STARTS ON ITS FIRST TICK, NEVER AT CONSTRUCTION — and it advances by RENDERED time, not wall time** | Two halves of one rule, and they pull against the standing "write the START value in the constructor" note rather than replacing it: the VALUE cannot wait (a frame between "asked for" and "showing its start value" is a frame of the END state) and the CLOCK must. Stamping `System.nanoTime()` in the constructor assumes construction and the first frame are adjacent, which holds for every gesture made while the loop is running and fails for the one that matters most — a host builds its screen and opens its first window OUTSIDE the frame loop, then constructs an editor, connects a workspace and compiles shaders before a frame is drawn. By the first tick the whole 150ms had elapsed, so the animation completed on that tick having rendered nothing: `ticks=0 over 0ms`, and "it just opens instantly" on screen. Every later gesture was perfect, so it read as the open animation being broken rather than as a clock that had run out before anyone looked at it. **And rendered time is the other half**: the first windows of a session open into the worst stall there is, measured at 398ms and 282ms between consecutive frames, so wall time charged a 150ms gesture its entire duration for frames nobody saw. An animation therefore HOLDS at its start value until it sees one ordinary frame gap, and a gap over 100ms thereafter advances it by nothing — bounded, so a host that never settles still finishes |
| **A WINDOW ANIMATES ITS LIVE CONTENTS, NOT A PHOTOGRAPH OF THEM — measured, not assumed** | Drawing a snapshot for the animation's duration is what DWM, Quartz and Mutter all do, and the reasoning for porting it was sound: `opacity < 1` sends `the paint walk` down the layer-FBO path, re-rendering the whole window every frame purely so it can be faded. It bought **nothing**. The same minimise measures **49 ticks at a mean 8ms gap with the photograph and 49 ticks at 8ms without it** — because the choppiness it was written to cure was never the layer FBO, it was the clock in the row above. What the photograph did buy was four separate defects, each of which looked like a different feature being broken: a **whitened** window (a layer FBO is premultiplied, so fading it needs RGB and alpha scaled together — the quad's alpha alone leaves the source at full brightness and washes it out), a **blank** one (the capture re-enters `the paint walk`, so without a `ctx.mirroring()` guard it photographs its own empty target), an **invisible** one (the capture inherits the animation's own transform and opacity — `the paint walk` pushes the element's transform even when the ambient pose has been cleared, so an opening window was photographed at a tenth scale and then scaled down again), and a **visibly different** one (a photograph is a one-shot while a window's contents are still arriving — a dock builds its groups in `tickFrame`, a frame later, so the picture was missing its tab strip, Problems panel and status bar). **`WindowSnapshot` itself stays**: a MINIMISED window is detached and has nothing left to draw, so its taskbar preview genuinely needs a picture taken while it was still whole — which is the one job it was written for |
| **...and every one of those was invisible on a minimise and a close, which is why they took four rounds each** | The two gestures anybody tests first start from a NEUTRAL transform at FULL opacity, so a photograph of them is correct however wrongly the capture is written. Only an open and a restore-from-minimise start scaled and transparent, and only they can expose it. The same asymmetry runs through the whole path — a restore reads its flight geometry a frame before its reattached node has been laid out, and a minimise cannot, because a window is fully laid out on the way out |
| **A design system's CONTROL tokens are not a window manager's timings, and a minimise that does not TRAVEL is not a minimise** | Two separate ways of getting motion wrong while the mechanism is perfectly correct. WinUI's `ControlNormalAnimationDuration` governs a button's hover; a window crossing a screen at that speed reads as a disappearance, which is why GNOME's minimise is **400ms** against Fluent's 167. The same applies to the curve: Fluent says things LEAVING should accelerate, which is right for a control getting out of the way and wrong for a window — an exit that speeds up is gone before the eye follows it, so a real window manager decelerates everything. And magnitude has a floor: a close scaling to 0.92 is below the threshold where the eye reads a scale as motion at all, which is exactly the "does nothing significant" report; GNOME uses 0.8. The travel rule is the sharpest of the three, because the failure was a *fallback*: with no taskbar button to aim at, the animation gave up on the movement and played a plain fade. Backwards — the entire information content of a minimise is WHERE THE WINDOW WENT, and GNOME hits the same case and still aims at a corner of the monitor. DWM's own numbers are published nowhere, so GNOME Shell is the only production window manager whose constants can honestly be ported |
| **`Transform.IDENTITY` is an EMPTY function list, so animating to or from it does not interpolate — it SNAPS at the halfway point** | CSS lerps two transforms component-wise only when their function lists line up, and `TransformProperty.interpolate` implements exactly that: `if (a.size() != b.size()) return snap(...)`, where `snap` is `t < 0.5 ? from : to`. An empty list lines up with nothing. Every window animation used `IDENTITY` as its resting end, so **none of them interpolated at all** — each held still and then jumped, once. It surfaced as four unrelated-sounding complaints: a maximise that "does nothing significant" (one jump), a close that is "choppy, I can see the individual frames" (the fade lerped, the scale popped), and a minimise that "just fades in place" — that last because the jump lands at `t = 0.5`, by which point `OUT_EXPO` has taken opacity to about 0.03, so the travel happens while the window is already invisible. **Nothing else could see it**: durations right, targets right, driver ticking every frame, `isAnimating` true, and dragging a window stayed perfectly smooth throughout because a drag writes `left`/`top` rather than a transform. Write both ends with the same functions — `scale(1,1)` and `translate(0,0) scale(1,1)` — which is what `TransformProperty`'s own javadoc already advises. `WindowAnimationEndpointsTest` asks the interpolator at **t = 0.25**, the one place a lerp and a snap differ |
| **What makes individual frames visible is PEAK VELOCITY, not duration or frame rate** | `OUT_EXPO`'s velocity at t=0 is **6.93x** its average; `OUT_QUAD`'s is 2x. Over a 400ms minimise at 60Hz that is 25% of the journey in the first frame and 44% by the second — and a minimise crosses a large part of the screen, so those are ~100px and ~75px of displacement between consecutive frames, far enough apart that the eye resolves them as separate positions rather than motion. The curve is mathematically smooth either way; what the eye samples is the STEP SIZE. Reported as "still choppy, I can see its individual frames" while the close and the maximise beside it — identical machinery, same frame rate, `OUT_QUAD` — read as smooth, which is what rules out every mechanical explanation. So the rule is by DISTANCE, not by direction: anything travelling far gets an even curve, and expo is kept only for an opening window, whose whole journey is a scale within its own box. GNOME gives its minimise expo and gets away with it on a compositor with motion blur and vsync-aligned presentation |
| **ONE kind of motion gets ONE mechanism, or the two drift and only one of them is ever debugged** | A taskbar preview rose with a transform-and-fade and slid between entries with a position animation, split because the rise "is the panel arriving at a position it already has" and so should leave `AnchoredPlacement`'s placement unfought — a constraint that did not apply, since nothing else writes that panel's position, which is exactly why the slide was allowed to animate it. The two then behaved differently on identical content: a slide onto a minimised window's entry was smooth while a rise onto the same entry snapped partway through. The fix was not to debug the second mechanism but to delete it |
| **Adding a child from inside `onWindowChanged` or a refresh inserts a Taffy node into a parent whose children are still being registered** | `Index (is 1) should be < child_count (0)`, the crash `UINode.taffyChildIndex` is named after, reached from a new direction: `Taskbar.onWindowChanged` calls `refresh()`, so building anything lazily from inside entry construction runs during the taskbar's own attach. Build a widget's own children in its CONSTRUCTOR — all of them, before attach — and resolve whatever they need lazily instead. It is the second time this session an existing documented hazard was met by creating a new instance of the case it covers |
| **A driven animation must write its START value in its constructor, not on its first tick** | One frame between "asked for" and "showing its first value" is one frame of the END state — a visible flash at the beginning of every gesture. The ticker exists to advance an animation, not to begin it |
| **`transition: none` and `TransitionEngine.isAnimating` exist, and are no longer what the window animations use** | Both were added for the cascade-driven version and both are worth keeping on their own terms — `none` is real CSS and `parse` answers an empty list for it, which is already the shape `tryStart` reads; `isAnimating` is the DOM's `transitionend` as a per-frame READ rather than a callback, because a listener registered against a transition that is retargeted mid-flight fires for an animation nobody is waiting on. Neither is a window-animation mechanism any more |
| **A test that asserts an animation's INPUTS passes against a build where nothing moves** | Four tests checked that the right classes went on and came off, and all four were green while the cascade was declining to animate any of them — the classes were doing exactly what they were told. The only assertion that separates "animating" from "applied instantly" is whether a timeline is RUNNING (`WindowFrame.isAnimating`). Intermediate frames stay untestable — the driver advances on `System.nanoTime()` — but "is it playing at all", "did it start from its start value", and "did the teardown wait for it" are all reachable, and they are the three things that actually broke. **And the fixture has to open its window with animations OFF**, or the opening timeline is still running when the gesture under test starts and `isAnimating` cannot say which one it is reporting |
| **"Animations off" has to turn off the WAITING too, or every caller learns to wait** | An animation that merely finished instantly would still defer the hide or the destroy by the frames the staging costs, so `requestClose` would be asynchronous in a mode where — as far as the user is concerned — nothing is animating. `WindowAnimator` runs its continuation synchronously when disabled, which is what lets every existing test go on asserting immediately. **And tests must disable it**: `TransitionEngine` advances on `System.nanoTime()` and cannot be stepped, so a live transition is asserted against wall time — and a live transform is a *real* transform, since hit-testing walks the same chain the paint does, so a test pressing a caption mid-animation misses it by an amount that depends on the machine |
| **A held-modifier gesture polls the modifier; it never listens for the key-up** | A key-up listener has to know that Alt is two keys, that a modifier can be released while the focus owner is somewhere else entirely, and that the release may not be delivered at all if the host took focus meanwhile. Masking the live `getCurrentModifiers()` against the mask the invoking chord carried answers all three without knowing any of them, and it is what GNOME's `switcherPopup.js` does with its `modifier-change` handler. The mask is read from `Keymap.acceleratorFor(command)` rather than hardcoded, so remapping moves the modifier that holds the gesture open with it — a hardcoded Ctrl leaves a user who rebound to `Alt+Grave` with a switcher that commits the instant it opens. **And SHIFT is stripped from the mask**: it is the direction qualifier in every switcher there has ever been, so letting go of Shift to cycle forwards again would otherwise commit mid-gesture |
| **A desktop-scoped close watcher is UNREACHABLE while any window is active** | `getTopCloseWatcher` asks the active frame's stack first and a frame registers as its own last watcher, so the frame itself always answers before anything registered outside a window. Escape aimed at desktop chrome therefore minimises or closes the window behind it instead. Anything that must take Escape at desktop level belongs on the **live-drag rung** in `consumeKeyboardEvent`, ahead of the cascade — which is the honest description of a window switcher anyway: it is held open by a modifier that is still down, so while it is up Escape can mean nothing else |
| **"Is this panel on screen" has ONE answer, and deriving a second from the region host can only ever see a DOCKED one** | `WorkbenchSession.captureOpenToolWindows` wrote `visible` from `host.showing(state.side())`, which a FLOATING or WINDOWED tool window can never satisfy — it lives in a frame, not in a region half — so every float recorded as closed and none ever came back. **Its placement survived perfectly**, which is what made it read as "restore is broken" rather than as one field: reopening the panel by hand put it back at exactly the right size in exactly the right spot. `ToolWindowManager.isPanelOpen` already answers correctly for both presentations and its own javadoc warns against that very expression, for the split-region bug it was written for; the session reintroduced it independently. The standing rule that the host is the truth is about **which half** holds a docked panel — never about whether a panel is open at all |
| **CLICK-FOCUS LANDS ON THE FRAME BEFORE ANYTHING IS DISPATCHED, so "focus is already in this window" is true before the window has done anything about it** | `emitMouseDown` walks up from whatever was hit to the nearest ancestor that `focusesOnClick()`, and a `WindowFrame` is `CLICK_NOT_TABBABLE` — so a press on a caption focuses the FRAME, and only then is the press dispatched. `restoreFocus`'s `contains(focused)` guard read that as "leave it alone" and never ran the focus delegate: a window that looks focused whose CONTENT is not, which is a floating tool window whose rail button stays dark, because that button lights from focus being inside the panel's own container. The frame ITSELF must not count as focused-inside; a real control inside it still does, because that is somebody's deliberate target. **A test through `sendInputEvent` cannot see any of this** — it dispatches straight at an element and skips `emitMouseDown` entirely, so the first version of the covering test passed against the bug and only `consumeMouseEvent` at a POINT reproduced it |
| **An OWNED window has no desktop to activate against, and a press must still focus it** | `attachOwned` parents a frame into its owner's overlay slot and deliberately makes it no desktop citizen — no registry, no taskbar entry, no z of its own — so `Desktop.activate` early-returns on the null owner and the press did nothing at all. Focus is the whole of what activation means for a window with nothing to raise and nothing to announce. It matters most for a DRAG, which is how it was reported: a drag never completes a click, so the press is the only moment there is |
| **Going into a FRAME is leaving the region, and only the RESTORE path forgets to say so** | Putting a tool window in a frame reparents its container out from under the region host, and a host still recording an occupant it no longer contains answers `isEmpty() == false` — so `sync()` keeps the region in the split and its whole width stays behind as a blank column beside the editor. Undocking by hand never showed it: `setType` hides the panel while its type is still DOCKED, so `hidePanel` takes its docked branch and clears the half. A session restore hides nothing — it decodes a placement that already says WINDOWED and calls `showPanel` — and `hidePanel`'s early return for a windowed type means the clear is never reached. So the panel opens correctly, in the right place, and the space it used to occupy is simply gone from the editor. `showInFrame` releases the slot itself, which makes it true however the frame was reached. **Assert on `isEmpty()`** — the thing `sync()` reads — because asserting the panel is open passes against the bug: it genuinely is open |
| **A windowed thing asked for before the tree HAS a window fails silently, and a session restore is exactly when that happens** | `showInFrame` needs a `UIDocument` to open into and returned false without one, into `applyVisibility`, which ignores the result. A host may legitimately restore on its first frame — before anything called `UIDocument.init` — and the harness does: `restoreSession` runs above `uiWindow.init(...)` in the same method. The DOCKED path needs no window, so those panels came back and the failure looked partial rather than ordered. The element was fully in the tree, all the way to the root; it was the ROOT that had not joined a window yet, so a chain walk looks perfect and `document()` is still null. **The answer has to be given twice** — remembered, then replayed when the workbench joins a window — and the replay must be **deferred a frame**, because `onWindowChanged` fires DURING the attach walk and the regions root it has to ask may not be registered yet. Same rule `ProjectFileTree`'s deferred refresh follows: an attach is not a moment to build things in |
| **MOVING A CLASS DOWN TO FIX AN INVERTED DEPENDENCY LEAVES A JAVADOC LINK THAT RECREATES IT — and a constant-pool scan cannot see it** (M6.10) | Twice in one afternoon. `TextRange` moved from `ui.text` to `text` and arrived importing `ui.text.HighlightRegistry` for two `{@link}`s; `ClipboardActions` moved from `ui` to `core.data` and arrived importing `ui.data.UiDataKeys` for one `@see`. Both classes had **zero imports** before the move, so in each case the move CREATED the edge it was made to remove. A javadoc reference compiles to nothing, so the bytecode instrument this project prefers — *"a reference in the constant pool is the real question"* — sees an empty class and passes; the source still says the two layers know about each other, and the next person needing a real reference finds the import already sitting there. `LayeringTest.theHeadlessSideNamesNoUi` is therefore asserted on **imports**, which is the opposite of every other assertion in that file and the only instrument that can see this. Use `{@code Name}` across a layer boundary, never `{@link}` |
| **A PACKAGE CANNOT BE A SUBDIRECTORY OF A TIER IT RANKS BELOW — the prefix rule makes it fail against its own parent** (M6.10) | `widget/scroll` was nested as `widget/layout/scroll`, and `LayeringTest` cannot express that: `ClassReferences.matches` is a `startsWith`, so every reference `Scroller` makes TO ITSELF matches the rule `com/crystalgui/widget/layout/` — and scroll sits below layout, so eleven of the twelve reported offences were the package against itself. Loosening the match to let a sub-package off is the obvious repair and is worse: it would silently permit `scroll -> layout`, which is the one edge the ordering exists to forbid. The dependencies agreed with the flat shape independently — `widget/text/MarkupView` uses a `ScrollerView`, and `layout/scroll` imported **nothing** from `layout`, so the nesting had no dependency justification either. **Directory nesting is a claim about rank**, not a filing convenience |
| **A SHADOW ROOT IS A `UINode` AND NOT A `UIElement`, AND `allNodes()` HANDED EVERY ONE OF THEM TO THE CASCADE** (M6.10) | `UIDocument.allNodes()` is what `StyleEngine` re-matches, and it ended `if (shadow != null) collect(shadow, into)` — the shadow ROOT as well as its children. So every shadow root in the tree was matched against every selector in every sheet and cascaded into a full `ElementStyle` **that nothing ever read**: the box tree walks `composedChildren()`, where a shadow root is transparent by construction, so it is never laid out and never painted. One wasted entry per shadow-hosting widget INSTANCE, across the 23 widgets that may host one, and a sheet writing `shadow-root { }` matched it. **Nothing could observe it** — a cascade result nobody reads is indistinguishable from no cascade at all — so it survived M5 and M6 entire. What found it was the type split: once `ShadowRoot` stopped being a `UIElement`, `collect` stopped compiling. `ShadowRootIsNotStyledTest` is the standing form of that compiler error, and **its counter-control is the assertion that matters**: a fix that stopped descending into shadow trees at all passes the first test and unstyles every composite in the engine, because a part is reached through `::part()` and a part lives in a shadow tree |
| **...and the split's dividend is that three runtime guards became signatures** (M6.10) | `refuseAsChild`'s `child instanceof ShadowRoot` check is deleted because it cannot be passed one; `shadowRoot.addClass(...)`, `.setId(...)`, `.attachShadow()`, `.setFocusPolicy(...)` and `.scrollTo(...)` stop compiling. **A document is still checked at run time, deliberately**: `UIDocument extends UIElement` because ours is the root element as well as the document — the one documented divergence from the DOM, and the reason `ShadowRoot` is the ONLY bare node. `children` and the observer stay typed `UIElement` for the same reason and it is the NARROWER type, not a leak: both non-elements are ROOTS, so every child really is an element, exactly as `ParentNode.children` is an `HTMLCollection` of Elements while `Node.childNodes` is a `NodeList` |
| **`parent()` ANSWERS A NODE AND `parentElement()` ANSWERS AN ELEMENT — the web's `parentNode`/`parentElement`, for the web's reason** (M6.10) | A shadow root is a parent and is not an element, so a walk that wants to ask its parent something an element answers has to say which question it is asking. Measured before choosing: of 129 `.parent()` sites in `main` only **four** chained a method onto the result. The widening cost 63 sites across the tree and **every one is behaviour-preserving** — a shadow root's own `parent()` is already null, so those walks stopped there before and stop one step earlier now, and no predicate in any of them could match one. The two chains differ at exactly one place, which is why `Styleable.getParent()` (null at a shadow boundary, so an outside combinator cannot reach in) is NOT the chain a scoped sheet is found through |
| **A SHEET CAN BE SCOPED TO MORE THINGS THAN IT CAN STYLE — `StyleScope` is not `Styleable`** (M6.10) | A scope root is only ever WALKED TO: `StyleEngine` asks how many hops separate an element from it and ranks candidates by that distance. It is never matched, never asked for an id or a class, never given a cascade. So the set of things that can be a scope is strictly larger than the set that can be styled, and a shadow root is exactly the difference — scoping a composite's own sheet to it is the documented way that sheet reaches its parts and nothing outside them. Before the Node/Element split the distinction had nowhere to live and the parameter was typed `Styleable`, which *happened* to work; after it, `proximityOf` walking `getParent()` would silently find no scope from any part inside the tree, and the feature would have been lost with nothing failing |
| **A TEST FOR A CONDITION THE WALK CANNOT PRODUCE IS DEAD CODE THAT READS AS A FEATURE** (M6.10, **open**) | `Focus.scopeOf` walks COMPOSED parents and tested `at instanceof ShadowRoot && delegatesFocus()`. A shadow root is transparent in the composed tree — a part's `composedParent()` is the HOST, never the root — so the branch could never fire, before the split as well as after, and the method's own javadoc has promised *"a delegating shadow root"* the whole time. The split turned it into a compile error because the types are now provably disjoint. **Left out rather than repaired**: `scopeOf` answers a `UIElement` and a shadow root is not one, so the honest candidate is the host, and whether a delegating composite should be its own Tab scope is a behaviour question that needs a test, not a port |
| **AN ACCEPTANCE CRITERION SATISFIED BEFORE THE CHANGE IT ACCEPTS IS NOT SATISFIED — and nothing in `:core:check` compiles `mc1710`** | 6.9b's accept list named `runClient`, `serverSmoke` and the session probe, and all three had been run at 6.9a, *before* the deletion — which is the change that broke them. The old engine's `Ui` and `ElementRegistry` went with it, `CgUiScreen` imported the first and both probes called `ElementRegistry.bootstrapBuiltins()`, and the module did not compile for two milestones. Nothing said so, because no core task builds it: `:core:test`, `:core:headlessTest` and the harness were all green over a deletion that had broken the one module that can see the loader seam. The mechanical half is a checklist entry — **`:mc1710:compileJava` belongs in every verification sweep that deletes anything from `core/`** — and the general half is that a green core is evidence about `core/` and about nothing else, which the section above already says at length and which is easy to read as advice rather than as a property of the build graph |
| **A rebind nothing RE-ASKS FOR is machinery that can never fire** | `Mc1710Workspace.client()` rebinds when the connection has moved, and for a release it would have rebound nothing: `CgUiScreen` asks for the client exactly once, at editor construction, and `CrystalEditor` holds what it was given for the life of the screen. So the wire could be replaced, the guard could notice, and no code path would ever reach the guard — a retained window stays pointed at a dead router for good, with the whole repair sitting there correct and unreachable. **No headless test can see this**: the mechanism passes every one of them, because a test calls `rebind` itself. It is visible only by reading who calls the accessor, which is the loader-seam class of defect `serverSmoke` exists for and the reason the plan asked for an in-game half at all. The fix is the per-frame re-ask in `pump` — free when the connection has not moved, and ONE re-ask is enough for the whole tree precisely because the rebind preserves the workspace's identity: `Workbench`, `WorkspaceTreeSource` and `FileOperations` are all holding the same object |
| **A client-side memo of what the SERVER was told is a lie the moment the connection changes** | "I have already asked the server to watch this" is a fact about a *peer* rather than about this client — so after a reconnect it records promises the new peer never made, the watch is never re-issued, and change notifications stop **permanently** for exactly the files that were open: no error, no log line, and an editor that simply stops noticing edits made underneath it. `Workspace.rebind` re-issues every watch, and `WorkspaceReconnectTest.aReboundWorkspaceIsStillWatchingWhatItHadOpen` is the standing form of it — a test that only checks a rebound client can still READ passes against the bug, because reading is what still worked. Retention is what made this reachable — before hidden windows existed, a client and its connection died together and nothing ever held one across a reconnect. The rule generalises to every "I already told them" cache: on a rebind it is INTENT to be re-issued, never a record to be trusted. The same pass drops presence and capabilities (pushed state describing a server nobody is talking to) and keeps **etags**, because an etag is what a save quotes and the server re-stats before writing — a genuinely stale write then returns a conflict a user can act on, where a cleared etag makes saving a not-yet-re-read file a client-side programming error |
| **An etag is `mtime + size` and nothing about it is SERVER-SCOPED, so two worlds hand out the same one for different content** | Which makes the obvious content-cache test useless: rejoining the same world after a file moved produces a new etag, so the conditional read is bypassed and the cache is correct with or without any fix. The hazard is rejoining somewhere ELSE — the condition matches, the new server truthfully says nothing has changed since that etag, and a client still holding the old bytes shows the previous server's file under this server's name. `InMemoryFileSystem` makes it reproducible on purpose: its clock is a monotonic counter from 1, so the first write to each of two filesystems collides exactly |
| **A reconnect repairs the PROTOCOL immediately and the VIEW on the frame the view comes back** | Two different deadlines, and conflating them gets one of them wrong. A missed change notification is missed for good, so watches cannot wait for a window to be restored. A directory listing is the opposite: it is stale rather than lost, nothing can arrive to say so, and re-fetching it for a hidden window is precisely the invisible work a detached window is supposed to have stopped doing. The deferral needs no new mechanism — a drain ticker returns false when its element leaves the tree and `onLayoutChanged` registers it again on the way back, so "on show" falls out of the ticker contract rather than needing `persisted` plumbed through it |
| **A full-size overlay is safe to make HITTABLE exactly when it is `display: none` whenever it is not drawn** | The standing fear is right and is this codebase's most-repeated failure — a transparent element covering the root swallows every click and nothing on screen says why. But the cure is not permanent `setHitTest(false)`, which also means the overlay's own controls can never be used: the switcher's tiles need hover, a press and a close button, and a control you cannot aim at is not a control. What makes it safe is that the element takes no box at all for every frame it is not on screen — which for a switcher includes the whole 150ms delay and therefore the whole of a fast tap, so there is no window in which an INVISIBLE one can eat a click. `display` rather than opacity is doing double duty here: it is also what keeps a full-screen element out of the layer-FBO path |
| **A modal gesture that is not an ELEMENT gets no keys, because dispatch goes to whatever has focus** | A window switcher takes no focus by design — it would have to give it back to exactly the window it is about to leave — so every key it needs reaches the focused element instead, and a focused editor moves its caret with an arrow. GNOME takes a real modal grab for the life of the gesture. Here the answer is the rung a live drag already occupies: intercept ahead of dispatch, in `consumeKeyboardEvent`. **Intercept only the keys the gesture acts on** — swallowing everything is closer to a grab and is a much larger claim than the feature needs, and it would take the CHORD with it, which has to keep resolving through the keymap or repeating it stops going through the command and the gesture silently stops being rebindable |
| **A gesture's STATE changes on the press; only the picture waits for the animation** | Hiding is detaching and a detached subtree paints nothing, so a minimise genuinely cannot detach until its animation has finished — but deactivation rode along with the detach, which put the whole state change 400ms behind the gesture. The caption stayed lit, the taskbar went on highlighting the window, and anything reading "the active window" described one that was visibly flying into the bar. Every window manager treats the gesture as having happened when it was asked for and animates a window that has logically gone. **The close path is deliberately not symmetrical**: `dispose()` reads whether the window was active in order to hand the keyboard to the next one, so deactivating early there would leave *nothing* active after a close — minimise can move because minimise is documented to hand over to nobody. And a test can only see any of this with animations **ON**: disabled, the continuation runs synchronously and the two orderings are indistinguishable, which is why asserting the window is still `VISIBLE` is load-bearing rather than spare |
| **A STATIC "do this on open" flag on a `GuiScreen` must be CONSUMED, because `initGui` re-runs** | Minecraft constructs a fresh `GuiScreen` on every display, so a request made by the caller that opened it has nowhere to live but a static — and `setWorldAndResolution` calls `initGui` again on **every display resize**, which a windowed client does whenever the OS window changes. `CgUiScreen`'s F6 flag stayed true for the rest of the session, so every later `initGui` re-ran "bring the editor forward": minimise, hide or close a window and the next resize showed it again, ACTIVE, with nothing on screen to explain why. Reported as windows being **uncloseable**, and the giveaway was *"this doesn't happen in the harness"* — a harness scene has no `initGui` at all, so the whole class of defect is invisible there. **Neither a programmatic `minimize()` nor a synthesised click reproduces it**, which is what made it look like an input bug: the only thing that does is re-entering the host's own open path |
| **A first `openWindow` does not go through `show()`** | `Desktop.addWindow` registers the frame, hands it an owner and attaches it; `show()` is how a HIDDEN one comes back. They share nothing but the attach, so anything that must happen when a window appears has to be written at both — the entry animation was on `show` alone and simply never played for the case that matters most, a window opening for the first time |
| **"Every live window" and "every window worth SHOWING" are different questions, and `Desktop` sizes itself from the first** | A tool window belongs to a window rather than being one — Win32's `WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW`, IntelliJ's floating panels — so it is in no taskbar and no Alt+Tab, and the taskbar read *Welcome · Geometry · Crystal Editor · Inspector · Notifications* until it was. The obvious fix is to filter `WindowRegistry.windows()`, and it is a trap: `Desktop` decides whether to take up ANY SPACE from whether that list is empty, so a tool window alone on the desktop would collapse the surface and take itself down with it — the engine's own "an unused compositor must not swallow clicks" rule, firing in reverse. `taskbarOrder()`/`switcherOrder()` are filtered VIEWS beside the complete list, and eviction, `byKey` and the sizing check all keep asking the complete one |
| **An owner takes its owned windows down with it, and must remember WHICH — but a cascade is not a close** | Two halves, each silent. A `FLOATING` tool window is a child of the overlay slot, so hiding the owner detaches it for free; a `WINDOWED` one is top-level and simply stayed on screen over an empty desktop. The bookkeeping is unavoidable because on the way back "hidden because the owner went" and "hidden because the user closed it" are indistinguishable — re-walking the owner group resurrects panels somebody deliberately shut. **And `onHidden` must not fire during the cascade**: its one listener is `ToolWindowManager`, which reads it as the user closing the panel and records it shut, so minimising a window marked every panel on it closed and *the next session save wrote that down* — the window comes back and the panels do not. `WindowFrame.hidingWithOwner` gates exactly the emit, so the frame's own Hide button still means close |
| **A cascade written in `hide()` runs at the END of the owner's animation, because for a minimise `hide()` IS the continuation** — and it then detaches with no animation of its own | The gesture rule (*"state changes on the press; only the picture waits"*) firing a second time, in a shape that reads as a rendering bug rather than an ordering one: the window sails into the taskbar and its panels blink out of existence once it lands, 400ms later. So an animated departure cascades at **gesture** time and each owned window plays its own flight; `hide()` keeps a synchronous pass as the backstop for a direct API hide, which is documented to be synchronous. **The second pass must then SKIP what the first started** (`hidingWithOwner` doubles as the in-flight marker) or the continuation hard-hides everything at exactly the moment its own animation ends — the identical symptom, reintroduced from the other side. And a window with no taskbar button of its own aims at its **owner's**, because that is truthfully where the group went; without it a tool window falls back to a shrink-in-place that says only that it stopped existing |
| **A MODE that intercepts keys must take only the keys it ACTS on, and end on anything else without eating it** | Keyboard Move/Size has no element, so it sits on the rung a live drag and the window switcher already occupy — ahead of dispatch, because a focused editor moves its caret with an arrow. The temptation is to swallow everything for the duration, which is much closer to a modal grab than the feature needs: a mode nobody remembers entering then eats the keyboard with no way out. Windows ends the mode on the next unrelated action and lets that keystroke through, so the way out is *anything you do next*. **Escape restores from a captured ORIGIN, never by unwinding the steps** — a nudge that hit the clamp moved less than it asked for, so replaying the deltas backwards does not land where it started |
| **"Which modal blocked this click" is a question about the SCOPE pressed, and one modal makes every lookup look correct** | `getHoveredElement` answers null for a blocked hit — deliberately, since inertness must act as `pointer-events: none` — and null is also what bare desktop looks like, so being blocked has to be *shown* or a modal window is indistinguishable from a hung one. The lookup must ask the hit's own scope: with per-window modality, blaming the globally-topmost modal pulses a window the user is not looking at while the one they clicked stays silent, which is worse than saying nothing because it points somewhere. **A fixture with a single modal cannot see the difference** — scoped and global agree — so the covering test needs a modal in each of two windows |
| **A gesture that must work ANYWHERE inside a window takes the press in the CAPTURE phase — the bubble phase sees only what nothing else consumed** | Alt-drag is "drag the window from anywhere", which means over a button, an editor, a tree row — every one of which consumes a press. A bubble listener on the frame is therefore a listener for the gaps between controls, which is precisely the case Alt-drag exists to escape. Capture also has to `stopPropagation()`, or the window moves AND the thing beneath it is clicked. The modifier itself is read from the **desktop**, never named in the widget: Alt is contested territory (`TextField` refuses Alt chords, `MenuBarView` claims Alt+letter) and a widget is the wrong place to decide |
| **A snap zone is read from the POINTER, never from the window's own edge** | Dragging a wide window leftwards puts its edge at the boundary long before the hand gets there, so an edge test snaps windows nobody was aiming at an edge with — and a narrow window could never reach a band at all. Every desktop reads the pointer, and **that includes the top** — which was the one edge that did not. It took the frame's CAPTION HEIGHT as its band, on the sound argument that a thinner one is unreachable: the pointer rides inside the caption for the whole of a drag, and a window clamped so its caption never leaves the work area holds that pointer a whole caption below the border. What it bought was an edge triggered by the WINDOW — it fires when the window's upper lip touches the border, while every other edge waits for the hand. The constraint is answered where it arises instead: a frame lets its caption rise one caption-height ABOVE the work area **while a move is live** and withdraws the headroom on drag end, which is what Windows does. And a corner belongs to the top band, or the two side bands meet it at both ends and it becomes unreachable |
| **Fullscreen needs NO geometry of its own — it is maximise plus a hidden strip** | The taskbar is *laid out* as a bottom bar rather than overlaid, so the window layer's box IS the work area: hiding the strip re-flows the layer to full height and every maximised window follows it. Windows' own model (maximise respects the taskbar, fullscreen covers it) falls out of the flex column with no bar-shaped special case anywhere. What it DOES need is memory of how it was entered — F11 from a maximised window must come back maximised — and whether the strip returns must be asked of the whole registry, not of one remembered window, because two windows can be fullscreen at once |
| **`Button` activates on the LEFT button only, and it took a context menu on one to notice it ever did otherwise** | The activation listener checked `isWasPressTarget()` and nothing about which button, so a right-click and a middle-click pressed the button too — which no toolkit does. Invisible for as long as nothing put a second gesture on a button; the moment a taskbar entry got a context menu, right-clicking it opened the menu **and** activated the window underneath, so the menu appeared over a window that had just minimised itself out from under it. Keyboard activation is unaffected because `Input` synthesizes the pair with button 0 — which is the whole reason it fakes a click rather than calling into `Button`. Anything wanting another button asks for that event directly (the taskbar's middle-click close listens on mouse-DOWN), which is the honest way round: activation is one gesture, not a wider `onPressed`. **The counter-assertion is not a formality** — a guard written as "reject everything" passes the right-click test and makes every button in the application dead |
| **A TASKBAR ENTRY IS NOT INSIDE THE WINDOW IT STANDS FOR, so it must answer `WINDOW_FRAME` itself** | `DataContext`'s walk goes outward from the invoking element: from a caption button that reaches the frame in two steps, but from a taskbar entry it reaches the taskbar and then the desktop — which answers with the **active** window. So right-clicking a background entry and choosing Close closes whatever is in front, which is the worst failure available to a menu whose entire job is to name its subject. `Taskbar.Entry implements DataProvider` puts it ahead of the desktop in the walk, which is the standing rule that an element which answers must still win, and keeps the desktop's answer the last resort it was written to be. **A one-window fixture cannot see any of this** — every resolution answers the same frame — so the covering test has to activate the OTHER window first |
| **...and a taskbar route must open into the ENTRY's window, never the FRAME's — HIDE IS DETACH, so the frame is out of the tree for exactly the windows the route exists to reach** | `SystemMenu.showJumpList` began `UIDocument window = frame.document(); if (window == null) return;`, which is correct for its sibling `showFor` (built against the frame, positioned off the title bar's box, so a detached window has no system menu by that route anyway) and fatal here: a minimised window answers **null**, and the jump list — the one affordance whose whole purpose is reaching a window that is not on screen — returned having done nothing. Reported as *"right click only works when the window is not minimized"*, which is the symptom stated exactly; every visible window was fine, so the route read as correct and the fault looked like input not arriving. The taskbar entry is the reliable end of the pair, the strip being up whenever any of this can be clicked — the same reasoning `ContextMenu.attach` already records for presenting from the attachment site rather than the target. **The covering test must minimise first**: a fixture that right-clicks a VISIBLE entry passes against the bug, which is how the route shipped with a green suite — and the assertion has to be that a menu OPENED, not merely that the window was not activated, which is all the existing right-click test checked |
| **A window command is registered globally and resolves its subject from the CONTEXT — never `desktop.activeWindow()` at the call site** | Four routes reach the same operation (system menu via `Alt+-`, via a title-bar right-click, via a taskbar right-click, plus the palette), and each arrives with a different `source` element. Resolving through `WindowFrame.WINDOW_FRAME` is what makes all four correct at once; a call site that asks the desktop for the active window is right for three of them and silently wrong for the fourth, which is the one a user reaches for precisely *because* the window is not in front |
| **Restore and Maximize are TWO rows, not one whose label changes** | Win32's system menu has both, each greyed in the state where it does not apply, and that is worth copying rather than collapsing. A row whose text changes is a row that is never in the same place twice — the same reason both menu renderers dim rather than hide — and muscle memory is most of why a system menu is worth having. It also means a caption **button** cannot map onto one command id: the maximise button is a toggle whose glyph swaps, so routing it through a command would need a state check to pick which id, which is more indirection than calling the method it would have called |
| **A window that is not a descendant of anything cannot be attributed by WALKING — a torn-out editor belongs to a workbench by PANEL-REGISTRY IDENTITY** | `DockWindow` is a top-level desktop citizen, which is the whole point of it, so "which project does this hold?" has no answer in the tree. What ties it back is that its dock builds content from that workbench's `DockPanelRegistry`. Without the discriminator a save sweeps up every dock window on the desktop and the restore opens another project's editors into this one. **And the covering test needs a non-empty stranger**: a `DockWindow` holding nothing destroys itself on the frame after a drag ends, so a fixture built around an empty leaf is gone before the save runs and passes with or without any filter at all |
| **A torn-out editor window is in NEITHER record unless something puts it there** | `DockLayout.tearOut` removes the leaf from the layout the session saves, and `DockArea.tearOutToWindow` builds a frame with no `key()`, which `Desktop.applyPersistedGeometry` skips. So the window was persisted by nothing and did not even come back docked — while the file's caret and scroll survived perfectly, because `openPaths()` reads the document store and that is document-level. **View state with no tab to land in is what makes it read as an editor bug rather than a persistence gap.** The corollary outlives the fix: `openTabPaths()` walks the MAIN dock only, so "what is open" has two answers and only one of them knows about torn-out windows |
| **`contains` on a Gson-serialised record is vacuously true for any string with an apostrophe** | Gson HTML-escapes by default, so `'` is written as `'` and a test asserting a record does NOT contain `"Someone else's"` passes against every implementation including one that writes it. Survived two mutation rounds looking like a filtering bug. Assert on a string with no `'`, `<`, `>` or `&` — and pair every "is absent" with an "is present" positive control, or a writer that produced nothing would satisfy it too |
| **A test asserting `isAnimating()` must open its window with animations OFF, or it passes against no fix at all** | Already recorded for the window-animation tests and paid for again immediately: with animations on, the tool window's own OPEN timeline is still running when the gesture under test starts, so `isAnimating()` answers true whatever the gesture did. Open the fixture with them disabled, enable for the gesture, restore in a `finally`. Found by a mutant, not by the suite — the test was green against the deleted fix |
| **ONE FACT, ONE RECORD — and when two records claim it, the writer that runs LAST wins silently** | A windowed tool window's geometry was in the per-PROJECT session (`ToolWindowState.floatingBounds`) and in the per-HOST desktop record (`desktop.<id>.json`, under its `toolwindow:` key). `showInFrame` applies the project's bounds and *then* calls `openWindow`, which is where the desktop record is applied — so opening a second project put its Inspector wherever the first project had left it. `FLOATING` was unaffected, because `attachOwned` never reaches `addWindow`, so it presented as only SOME of them being wrong: a placement bug, not a duplicated record. Both halves are needed — stop writing them, **and refuse to apply a record that already has them**, or the bleed outlives the fix by as long as somebody's config file does. A key is what a window can be NAMED by; it is not a claim on any particular record |
| **A tool window's frame is built per show and DESTROYED per hide, so asking the frame about a closed panel asks a corpse** | `hideFrame` removes it from the map and calls `destroy()`, because what survives a hide is the placement record and not the window. A test asserting `WindowState.HIDDEN` after a user close therefore gets `DESTROYED` — the state is right and the question is wrong. Ask `ToolWindowManager.isPanelOpen`, which is also what the session record reads |
| **A window is activated a frame BEFORE content that builds on a deferred rebuild exists** | `openWindow` raises and activates, and activation focuses — but a `DockArea` builds its groups in `tickFrame`, deliberately never inline. So at the one moment `focusDelegate()` is asked, `activeGroup()` is null, there is no tab and no content, and the delegate falls back to the dock itself: the bug it exists to prevent, arriving through the back door. The answer has to be given **twice** — at activation, and again the first time the layout says there is something to give it to. Found only by printing the delegate's inputs; a fix that reads correctly and never fires looks identical to no fix |
| **A drop must activate and focus what it received, and that belongs in `performDrop` — not in the Drop listener** | Two things were missing and they present as one. The receiving dock's `activeGroup` was never set, so `activePanel()` — and `activeFilePath()`, the Problems panel, the breadcrumbs and `Ctrl+S` through it — went on answering about whatever was there before: docking an editor back left the Problems panel reading *"No file is open"* with the file plainly on screen. And nothing focused it, so the tab arrived selected but **cold**, drawing its unfocused decoration tint and refusing keystrokes. The cross-window case is what makes it unmissable rather than untidy: the window the tab came from destroys itself once empty, and that takes the focus owner out of the tree. On the listener it covers only a drag, leaving a command or a menu dropping a panel nobody is working on — and leaves the only testable seam unable to see it, since a fixture cannot easily stage a drag ACROSS windows (the desktop's window layer is what the pointer resolves against) |
| **A drag never completes a click, so the thing being dragged is never SELECTED** — select on the press | The pointer moves, so no Up lands on the element the Down went to, and the selection a click would have made never happens. Dragging an unselected tab therefore lit **two** tabs at once — the selected one, and the one being carried, which click-focus had just outlined — and dropped it as a background tab. Both references select on press. Safe to do mid-dispatch here only because `DockGroup.sync` rebuilds its strip when the panel LIST changes and not when the selection does; the general rule against rebuilding what you are being clicked on is unchanged |
| **The thing being dragged leaves the list it is being inserted into, and a gap the size of IT stands in the cell it left** — `InsertionMarker.withdraw`/`restore` | Three rules, each silent when broken, which is why they are the widget's and not each caller's. Leaving the item in shows it twice, once where it is and once where it is going — and a hidden item still in the list has no box, so every midpoint test after it answers against a cell that is not there and the index stops moving as you drag past it. The gap must open **immediately**, in the cell just vacated, or hiding collapses the list by one and a pointer that has not moved is suddenly in its neighbour's cell. And it is `display: none`, never a detach: `Input.forgetElement` cancels a drag whose source leaves the tree. The gap takes the WITHDRAWN item's measured size rather than a neighbour's, which is invisible in a stripe of identical 20px buttons and wrong for every tab, since two filenames are never the same width |
| **A tooltip must never show while a drag is live, and it is the TOOLTIP's rule, not each widget's** | `startDrag` takes pointer capture, and the spec treats everything during capture as inside the capturing element — which is what stops `:hover` flickering across everything a drag crosses. The cost is that the source stays hovered, so its delay elapses *in mid-air* and the tip appears beside what you are carrying, over the strip you are aiming at. Checked at all three moments one can reach the screen — both entry points and every frame of the placement ticker, because a drag can begin under a tip that is already up, which neither entry point would be called again for. The stripe rail hid its own on mouse-down and no other widget knew it had to |
| **A band derived from a hideable element vanishes with it** — `DockGroup.stripBandContains` read the FIRST TAB's box | Fine until a tab could be withdrawn for the length of a drag, at which point dragging the only tab in a group made the strip's band zero-height: the drop fell straight through it and offered to SPLIT the pane instead of reordering. The strip's own box is both the fix and the more honest band, since it includes the scrollbar row reserved under the tabs |
| **`ElementStyle.getComputed` answers NULL for a property nothing has written** — the initial value is not a candidate | So an element that has never been touched reports neither `FLEX` nor `NONE`, and a test asserting the default passes only by accident. Ask the question that every state can answer — "is it hidden" — not "what is its display" | **`ComputedStyle` (M5 5.2) is the answer that never nulls**: `ElementStyle.computed()` is an immutable snapshot where an unwritten property answers its initial and an inheritable one its parent's, cached with the parent's snapshot as part of the key. The box tree reads that; the old engine keeps reading `getComputed`
| **A SHARED CAPTURE IS SHARED, NOT WHOLE-SURFACE** — the backdrop is the union of what ASKS for one, padded by the blur's reach | One capture per frame that every consumer crops out of is the right design and is what stops a second glass element costing a second target switch. Taking "shared" to mean "the whole screen" is the step that silently follows it, and it made glass a frame killer: a taskbar island is about a fortieth of the screen and was paying for a full-surface scene blit, a full-surface MSAA RESOLVE (the most expensive thing in the path, since it touches every sample it covers) and a full-surface composite per enclosing layer, every frame — a 120fps desktop at 50–70. Region-scoped it is `792x79`, **1.8% of the screen**, and the desktop holds 120fps with glass live. Seed the region from the PREVIOUS frame's union so a settled UI captures once for all consumers, and widen it immediately when a consumer is not covered, so the answer stays exact and only the cost is speculative. **The targets stay screen-sized and a SUB-RECT is used** — resizing them mid-paint is the incomplete-framebuffer hazard `CgUiBackdrop.prepareFrame` exists to avoid, and a corner is free because the sub-rect is the same FRACTION of every target, so a normalised UV and the blur's step maths need no change |
| **A SCISSOR RECT IS IN SCREEN PIXELS, so a target with its OWN coordinate space must not inherit one** — `CgUiBackdrop.withoutScissor` | Every ordinary layer FBO is screen-sized, which is why `beginLayerFbo` is right NOT to reset the clip: an element inside an `overflow: hidden` subtree must stay clipped when it is promoted. The backdrop capture and the blur targets are the first things here that render into a target sized differently from the screen, and for them the inherited rect is not a clip but a coordinate error. Glass drawn inside a clipped subtree got blur targets whose left strip was never written — the stage's clip began ~180px into a 1920px screen, and the same 180 landed 180px into a 480px target, so 37% of it stayed at the clear instead of 9%. Panels over that strip sampled transparent black and came out DARK, with a hard edge exactly where the rect began, growing with the radius because a wider kernel reaches further into the hole. **It presents as a sampling or clamping fault and is neither**: the arithmetic downstream was correct the whole time, it was being handed a target with a hole in it. Six rounds went into the sampling. A readback of the target found it in one |
| **A `Pass` helper must be declared ABOVE `void vertex`, and a shader that FAILS TO COMPILE still runs its pass** | The material compiler hoists the region of a Pass body above `void vertex` into both generated stages; anything written between `vertex()` and `fragment()` reaches the vertex source only. A fragment helper placed there fails the FRAGMENT compile with `undefined variable "cg_tap"` — and every property it touches is reported undefined with it, so the error list reads as though the Properties block were broken rather than the layout. The second half is what makes it expensive: a failed compile is not a failed draw. The pass still switched targets, still cleared, and still submitted, so the blur targets came out part clear-black and part fallback-white — on screen a panel with dark regions and blown-out ones, indistinguishable from a sampling bug. **The compile error was in the harness log from the very first run**, and was never read because the log was tailed rather than searched |
| **AN 8-DIGIT HEX IN A SHEET IS `#RRGGBBAA` — alpha LAST — and the engine's int is `0xAARRGGBB`; a pin written in the int order is a DIFFERENT COLOUR at a DIFFERENT ALPHA, with no error** | `--taskbar-glass-tint: #EB1C1D21` meant (28,29,33) at 92% and parsed as pure red (235,28,29) at 13%, so the taskbar's Mica was a red wash over the blurred desktop — measured at (52,25,25) over a (25,25,25) editor, which is that mix to the digit. **It was written in the int order TWICE**: that pin replaced a shipped `#E01F2023`, which parses as (224,31,32) at 14% and had been washing the bar red since it was first authored, so the rewrite reproduced the fault it was standing on rather than inheriting it. Both preview pins and both themes' `--surface-glass` had the same slip. It survived because it still looked like *a* material, and two comments were written blaming saturation and the harness's red clear colour before anyone read the parser (`ColorValue`, `case 8: // #RRGGBBAA`). The tell is an alpha that makes no sense for the role: a 13% *base* surface, a 19% veil that reads as a colour. Every other 8-digit pin in the themes was already CSS order, which is what made the four wrong ones invisible in a list |
| **A `withMaterial` body must not FLUSH — the properties it sets are uploaded by the bind AFTER it** | `applyProperties` is CPU-only and `withMaterial`'s second `useMaterial` is what uploads; a `ctx.flush()` inside the body draws the queued quads against whatever the PREVIOUS bind uploaded. The gradient's first version drew a quad per stop segment that way, and every segment took the colours of the segment before it — a wrong picture with no error, and one a single-segment gradient cannot show. Set properties inside the body (as `CgUiRoundedRect` does) or before the call (as `CgUiGlass` does), submit, and let `withMaterial` flush. The corollary is that a drawable needing several property sets is several `withMaterial` calls, which is what pushed the gradient to evaluate all its stops in ONE draw |
| **THE HOST'S FIXED-FUNCTION ALPHA TEST IS ON, AND IT DISCARDS EVERY UI FRAGMENT UNDER 10% ALPHA** — `Minecraft.startGame()` sets `glEnable(GL_ALPHA_TEST)` + `glAlphaFunc(GL_GREATER, 0.1)` and leaves it there | A compatibility profile applies that test to programmable-pipeline draws too, and NOTHING on our side models it — `CgRenderState` carries blend, depth, cull and stencil and no alpha — so a material neither sets it nor clears it and whatever the host left on governs every quad, glyph, gradient and glass surface. Not dimmed: **cut**, with a hard edge exactly where the alpha crosses the reference. Measured on the taskbar's glow, `linear-gradient(90deg, transparent 18%, #3574F033 50%, transparent 82%)` across a 1999px bar: a hard-edged band from x=642 to x=1347 with the ramp INSIDE it exactly right and both ends cut at α≈0.105. So axis, stop positions, premultiplied interpolation and the one-draw window were all correct and the picture was still wrong — six of the seven things one checks first are the gradient's. **The harness cannot see it by construction**: LWJGL3 has no fixed-function alpha test to leave on, so the identical CSS is correct there and no scene, probe or readback can reproduce it. **What it reaches is a fragment's OUTPUT alpha, never an alpha in the CSS**: a `glass()` tint at 7.5% is an input to a mix inside the shader and the surface still writes its coverage, so the acrylic panels were never affected. The ones that are: a `background-color` at or under 10% (Fluent's subtle fills, 6% and 3.5%, discarded whole), the transparent shoulder of any gradient, the outermost sliver of every anti-aliased SDF edge, and the opening frames of any layer composited at a low opacity — so it reads as "the translucent parts are missing" or "the soft edges are hard", never as one GL flag. **Disabled TWICE per frame**, because `endFrame` closes the frame's scope before the composite: the frame's disable protects each element's fragments, the composite's protects the finished picture, which is clipped by ACCUMULATED alpha and would take a 7% panel away whole rather than cutting its shoulders |
| **A STATE SLOT WITH A STUB GETTER IS WRITE-ONLY, AND LOOKS EXACTLY LIKE A WORKING ONE** | A widget with a setter and no reader tempts a contract slot written as `item -> false`. It is settable by a server, never written to the wire, and declared in a way that reads as complete — so the state simply never travels, and the widget arrives at its default with nothing to search for. Three slots were written that way in one sitting (`MenuItem.checkable`/`accelerator`, `SearchField.placeholder`/`notFound`) and all three were found by a coverage test asserting that a stateful widget writes SOMETHING after being changed, not by reading the code. The getters now exist. The mirror case is legitimate and must be spelled out where it occurs: `TextField.value` is read-only on purpose, because it is derived from the text and applying it would apply the same thing twice |
| **DECLARATION ORDER IN A CONTRACT IS APPLY ORDER, and four widgets depend on it** | The hand-written `readState` methods encoded their ordering in statement order, where nothing could see it and nothing could check it. `Slider` must take its range before its value or the value is clamped against the old bounds — a slider arriving with range 0–100 and value 80 lands at **1** if the previous range was 0–1, silently, and only for a widget whose range the server also changed. `ColorSelector` must take mode, then original, then colour, because `setInitialColor` moves the live colour with it. `Dropdown` must have its options before an index into them means anything. `TextField` must have its mode before its text, or the text is parsed by the old one. `WidgetContractRoundTripTest` asserts each of the four against a FRESH instance, which is the only assertion that cannot pass against a contract whose slots are reordered |
| **"CAN THIS REPORT X" AND "WAS THIS ASKED TO REPORT X" ARE DIFFERENT QUESTIONS** | The first is a fact about the widget CLASS and belongs on its contract; the second is a session's subscription and is per instance. Collapsing them makes every client attach a listener for everything its widgets are capable of and report to nobody. `getReportableEvents()` is the contract's, `getReportedEvents()` the subset a session asked for — and the second is still a field on the element only because the encoder that writes it is a context-free `Codec<UINode>`, which M2's mirror fixes. Before contracts there was one set and no validation at all: a session could ask any element for any string, the request was described, and the client's wiring hit a `default` arm that logged *"which this client cannot observe"* and carried on |
| **A WIDGET DECLARES ITS OWN RATE, because the right answer is a property of the INTERACTION** | A `TextField` fires per keystroke and a `Slider` per pixel of drag, so a server-side panel that simply forwards them sends a packet per keystroke — and the handler author has no way to know that without reading the widget. Phoenix LiveView puts `phx-debounce`/`phx-throttle` in the markup for the same reason. **`commitOnRelease` is the half that makes throttling safe**: dropping intermediate values is fine and dropping the LAST one is data loss, so a slider released between ticks would otherwise report a value that is not where it ended up |
| **A LEAF WITH A `MeasureFunc` WAS MEASURED AGAINST ITS CONTAINER'S MAIN SIZE, NOT ITS OWN, UNDER `flex-wrap: wrap`** — Taffy's `determineHypotheticalCrossSize` read `isWrap ? NaN : targetSize`, and the branch is now **deleted** in the vendored fork | The leaf's **width stayed correct** and only its height was short, so the symptom is clipped text rather than a box that looks wrong. Measured on a 200px row of two `flex-grow: 1` text leaves: `nowrap` told the measure function **100** and `wrap` told it **200**, so the leaf reported a height for a width it was never given. `UIText` avoids the whole path with a post-layout recompute and its javadoc named this defect as the reason, ending *"not something fixable without forking a third-party Maven dependency"* — the fork is now `taffy/`. **The branch was not protecting the aspect-ratio case it was written for, it was destroying it**: four of five AR shapes in a wrapping row came out **zero-high**, where `nowrap` gave the right answer.
