| **A child-side class may name only JDK types, `com.crystalgui.text.*` and the bridge — a shared utility is MOVED, never imported across** | The band loader is child-first over `com.crystalgui.language.*` and carries our own class files, so a child-side class importing `language.java` does not fail: it quietly defines a SECOND copy on the far side of the bridge, and two objects of "the same" type stop being assignable. `SimilarNames` was imported that way for a release — harmless, being stateless, and exactly the precedent not worth having. The rule is mechanical: **a class that imports `org.mozilla.javascript` is child-side**. It used to be a TABLE in `js/package-info.java`, because the `Js`/`Rhino` prefixes stopped saying which is which the moment the fix catalog turned out to need the AST — and a table is a thing that rots: it had `JsCompletionProvider` on the wrong side (it imports `java.TypeIndex` and `run.ScriptPolicy`, so it is host) and hedged on six classes with "loaded there in practice". It is now the **directory**: `js.host` and `js.rhino`, one rule each. The six hedged classes were settled by asking who calls them — every caller is child-side, so they are too |
| **A runtime's report describes the text that RAN, and must be converted against it** | A run takes time and the file is edited while it runs, so a row and column the engine reports mean something only against the source it compiled. Converting them against the buffer as it is now moves the squiggle one row per line typed above — the "the conversion is only legal against the document the analysis saw" rule, broken by the one lane added after it was written. The compiled source travels with the compilation; a report whose text has since changed is dropped, because the next run will speak for itself |
| **`Integer.MAX_VALUE` is how `Diagnostic.onRow` spells "to the end of this line", so a column is clamped BEFORE it is added to an offset** | `at + column` overflows to a negative number and the whole mark collapses to a point, which reads as a diagnostic that was never widened rather than as arithmetic. `Rope.pointToOffset` has a test named for this; the runtime-problem lane became its second reader and repeated it |
| **Rhino's application class loader is one definition, and so is "does this class exist"** | The executor and the interop resolver each had their own spelling of the host loader, so the editor's "does this class exist" and the runtime's "load this class" were two questions with two answers about one name. `java.util.Map.Entry` is the case that exposed it: the source spelling is what a script writes, what the popup offers and what the Java probe resolves, while the JVM knows only `Map$Entry` — so the name the editor suggested threw when it ran. `JsLoaders` is the one lookup, and it retries in the nested spelling |
| **A membrane over an engine's wrapper must forward everything the engine dispatches on, not only what it renames** | `RhinoRemapping` translates member names, and the two things it nearly took away have nothing to do with names: `Wrapper`, because `NativeJavaMethod.call` unwraps its receiver through it (a `Scriptable`-only membrane is found by the lookup and rejected by the call), and `SymbolScriptable`, because that is how `Symbol.iterator` reaches a Java `Iterable` — so `for (x of javaList)` worked unmapped and stopped working the moment a mapping existed. A class object is also a `Function`, or `new java.util.ArrayList()` is "not a function". **And nothing downstream may then ask a value what CLASS of wrapper it is**: under a mapping nothing is a `NativeJavaClass`, so `Java.type('…')` was reported as an instance of `java.lang.Class` and printed as `class java.util.ArrayList`. Ask `unwrap() instanceof Class` |
| **The engine loader belongs on the thread for calls INTO the engine, and must come off for the script's own execution** | Rhino needs it up to `initStandardObjects`, which is where the regular-expression engine is resolved and cached. After that the script is calling OUT — into a mod's Java, into CrystalGUI — and leaving a child-first loader as the context loader makes every `ServiceLoader` in the application resolve against the engine's classpath, which is the same class of bug pointing the other way |
| **A completion fallback for "I cannot type this receiver" must fire on NO TYPE, never on an empty member list** | A receiver that resolved to `string` or `Array` and came back empty is a gap in `membersOf`, not an untypable receiver — so the popup offered every global the last run had left AS MEMBERS OF A STRING. The gap was real (the standard prototypes were never read; `getAllIds` is needed, since every prototype member is non-enumerable), and the fallback hid it: a list appeared, so nothing looked broken |
| **A line the GRAMMAR cannot parse decolours the whole file — and whether it does depends on a SEMICOLON** | `import a.b.C;` is what this engine supports and what tree-sitter reads as an ES module declaration expecting a string or a `from`. Measured on one fixture: **with** the terminator the error is contained to its own line and everything below is correct; **without** it — ordinary JavaScript, the language inserts semicolons and authors rely on it — the recovery reaches into the body and `var` two rows down is reported as a `variable`, `new` stops being a keyword. So a fixture written with the semicolon passes against no fix at all, which is how the first version of the test went green while the screenshot still showed the bug. `Grammar#sourceFilter` blanks the statement before the parser sees it — length-preserving, and `TreeSitterTokenizer` **throws** on a filter that is not, because every offset it reports is an offset into what it parsed |
| **...and that filter is declared on `Grammar`, not by the language whose dialect it is** — the dependency can only point that way | The instinct is to have `JsLanguage.register()` install it, and `ExecutionNeedsNoGrammarTest` forbids it: `language.js` — with `.java`, `.run`, `.map` and `.engine` — may not name `language.grammar` at all, because a dedicated server runs scripts and must not load five platform natives to do it. Which leaves `.grammar` naming `JsImports`, so that class is the one in `js.rhino` **both loaders define** — safe only because it is stateless and every value it hands over is a `String`; the moment `Scanned` or `Imported` crossed the bridge the two copies would stop being assignable. And the same blanking, not a second copy of the regex: the executor, the analyser and the grammar run the one `JsImports.blank` |
| **A Rhino `Loop` IS a `Scope`, so the structural "what statement am I in" walk stops on the loop’s own HEADER** | `ForLoop`, `WhileLoop` and `ForInLoop` all extend `Scope` — they have to, since `for (let i …)` declares into one — so a condition’s parent answers yes to "is my parent a statement container" and the walk returns the comparison instead of the loop. Two fixes were wrong at once and neither looked it: "Surround with try/catch" on `while (i < xs.length)` offered to wrap **the condition**, producing a `while` header containing a `try` block, and "Introduce variable" hoisted `xs.length` above the loop — evaluated once, so the loop never terminates. A **hang** from an edit the author accepted without reading. `JsQuickFixes.enclosingStatementOf` excludes `Loop` and is now the single walk both fix classes call; the class-based `Block`/`Scope` test was right and the inheritance underneath it was not |
| **A Rhino node’s reported LENGTH is not its text — the `KeywordLiteral` in `this.x` measures FIVE characters** | So reading the source span gives `"this."`, dot and all, and a comparison against `"this"` quietly never matches. The arrow-function conversion was therefore offered on every function that used `this` — precisely the case it exists to refuse, since an arrow inherits `this` lexically and the converted function reads a different object under the same name **and still runs**. Ask `RhinoTokens.keywordOf`, which calls `toSource()` and RENDERS the node rather than measuring it. That class was written for the `Token`-constant divergence and this is a second, unrelated reason to go through it: a span is a claim about the document, and only the node can say what it spells |
| **An operator predicate written as "ends with `=` and does not begin with one" excludes plain `=`** | Which is the case that matters. `xs[i] = 0` was therefore read as a FETCH, so the index loop converted to `for…of` and the body became `x = 0` — assigning to the loop variable and discarding it. The array stops being written, nothing throws, and the diff looks like the refactoring it was advertised as. List the assignment operators; there are sixteen and they are not going to change |
| **Parameter names SURVIVE COMPILATION — the belief that they do not sent a session looking at the wrong artefact** | `src.zip` is a JDK thing and ships with no JRE, so "real parameter names need attached sources" was taken as settled and the production case as lost. Measured: `ArrayList.add` carries `e` in its `LocalVariableTable`, `String.format` carries `format`/`args`, and our own jar carries its names today because Gradle passes `-g`. **Three traps, each silent.** Slot 0 is `this` for an instance method and the FIRST PARAMETER for a static one, and `String.format` is the static case — read it wrong and every name shifts by one into a signature that is plausible and authoritative. `long` and `double` take two slots each. And the table holds every local, not just the parameters: `ArrayList.add(int, Object)` reports `[this, index, element, s, elementData]`. **`-parameters` is not an alternative, it is the other half** — `MethodParameters` is the one attribute needing no `Code` attribute, so it is the only thing that can name an INTERFACE method, which for an SPI-heavy module is most of the surface |
| **`getJavadoc()` answers null until `doc.comment.support` is enabled — and the node covers the comment either way** | Which is the confusing half: `JavaSignatures` had to scan the text to step over a doc comment the accessor denied existed, and recorded that as a workaround. One option turns it on. **It is not a diagnostic switch** — doc-comment *parsing*, not the ~40 javadoc problems, which have their own options and stay off. And **an override must inherit its comment or the feature looks broken on arrival**: an overriding method usually carries `@Override` and nothing else, so without walking to the supertype a large fraction of methods render an empty body, which reads as the feature not working rather than as the method having nothing to say |
| **Retained memory measured as a HEAP DELTA is mostly churn; measured by nulling the reference is zero — measure it by SCALING** | Both are wrong and neither looks it. A delta bracketing a build counts every `ZipFile` structure and short-lived string the collector has not reached: the type index read **~14MB for 11k entries**, about 1.3KB per record of three strings, which would have put it at four times §7.3's budget. Nulling the local and re-measuring reads **~0KB**, because the caller's own variable still holds it. Build several and hold them all — churn does not multiply and retention does, exactly. The real figure was **~331 bytes**, so a full index is ~18MB against a ~20MB budget |
| **`registerCommands` runs from `UINode`'s INSTANCE INITIALISER, so a per-INSTANCE registration placed there passes `null`** | Its own javadoc says fields are not initialised yet and calls the hazard deliberate, and it is still the shape that gets reached for, because the method reads as "where this widget wires itself up". `Workbench` contributed its `ProjectIndex` there, `ProjectSourcesRegistry.contribute` opens with `if (provider == null) return`, and the whole of M15 S4 was dead on arrival: every cross-file reference in the workspace reported *cannot be resolved to a type* while the registry, the name environment, the project tier and 2,533 tests were all correct. Nothing threw and nothing logged, because **"no provider" and "a provider that knows nothing" are the same answer from outside**. It is also once per CLASS, so even non-null it would register only the first workbench's index. Register per-instance things in the constructor; this hook may name statics only |
| **A bound method reference on a MUTABLE field captures the value, not the field** | `bufferSnapshot::get` binds whatever the field pointed at when the reference was created -- in a constructor, the empty map -- so every later snapshot is invisible to it. It compiles, it reads exactly like an accessor, and the failure is silent: the open-buffer tier never answered, so an unsaved edit lost to the file on disk. The two lines beside it were lambdas and worked. Write `path -> field.get(path)` whenever the receiver can be reassigned |
| **A project package must be reported in the UNNAMED module, never the classpath's** | `ScriptNameEnvironment.getModulesDeclaringPackage` answered for workspace packages by borrowing whatever module `java/lang` belongs to -- correct for the live tier, whose bytes really do come from the classpath, and wrong for a source file compiled here. On a JRT that is `java.base`, so `com.example.util` was attributed to `java.base` while `com.example` -- the compiled unit's OWN package -- was unnamed. **ECJ discards a package chain split across two modules without a word**: it never asks for the type, it just looks for the simple name in the current package. Reported as *The import com.example.util cannot be resolved* while this environment was answering that the package exists. It hides whenever the importer sits in an UNRELATED package, because then every segment is invented here and all of them are consistently wrong -- which is why a cross-package import test passed for a release while `com.example.Main` importing `com.example.util` did not |
| **A PLAYER IS NOT THE ENTITY THEY ARE WEARING — 1.7.10 builds a new `EntityPlayerMP` on every respawn and every dimension change** | `respawnPlayer` constructs a fresh entity, copies the old one into it, and `NetHandlerPlayServer` re-points `playerEntity` at the replacement; the socket, the channel and the handler are untouched, and every map keyed on the entity has just been orphaned. **Inbound dies permanently** — frames name the new body, the lookup misses, and every packet from that client is dropped for the rest of the session. **Outbound keeps working**, because the stale entity retains its handler reference and FML resolves the `PLAYER` target through it at send time, so deltas still arrive and the panel still animates: it reads as an input bug in the widget rather than an identity bug in a map. **And the cleanup misses too** — `PlayerLoggedOutEvent` carries the new body, so an entity-keyed removal removes nothing and the connection, its multiplexer, the workspace binding and the presence entry outlive the player. The identity is the `GameProfile` UUID; the ROUTE is the `NetHandlerPlayServer`, which is what outlives the entity and gets re-pointed, so a send **resolves** its target rather than capturing one. `Mc1710Peer` is both. No test can see any of this — it lives in FML's respawn path |
| **A PER-TICK "COPY THE MODEL INTO THE WIDGETS" METHOD IS A LIST YOU CAN FORGET TO ADD TO, AND FORGETTING LOOKS CORRECT** | The shape every server-side panel was written in — `mirror(model)` called from `tick()`. It works, and its failure is silent in the worst way: a widget nobody remembered to write keeps the value it was BUILT with, which is usually right, so the panel opens correct and then never moves. Nothing throws and nothing logs. Three further costs come with it — a nested model is walked by hand, a COLLECTION cannot be expressed at all (it can re-set what it has and cannot say "these forty items became forty-one"), and a window nobody is watching pays for the whole walk anyway, because `tick()` runs regardless. `ui.projection` replaces it: the pair is stated once, the engine compares and writes, and the set is skipped while no viewer is looking. **The cost was never the comparison** — one `equals` per DISPLAYED field, and a five-hundred-field model behind a twelve-control panel has twelve projections |
| **...and once the widgets PROJECT the model, the model holds NO LISTENERS FOR ITS VIEWS — a listener list on a domain object is the mirror() shape from the other side** | `MachineModel.onChanged` was the pre-projection mechanism: a panel subscribed so it could re-copy the model on change, and the model carried a list with unsubscribe handles "because one machine may have any number of panels watching it". After M3.P nothing subscribed and every setter still announced into an empty list — dead code whose javadoc called it "the one concession to the UI", which is what it was. A projection READS; the model's whole contract with the UI is that its getters answer, so a machine that outlives its viewers has nothing to accumulate and a second window costs it nothing. `EngineModel` lost the `Runnable` it existed to relay through, which is the tell: a callback threaded through a constructor purely to reach a listener list is the list's cost showing up one object away |
| **...and a projection must be SEEDED BEFORE THE DESCRIPTION IS TAKEN, or the window opens visibly wrong and corrects itself** | `open()` encodes the tree as it stands, so a projection that has only ever run from the tick loop leaves the first description carrying whatever the panel's CONSTRUCTOR built. Every projected field then arrives one state delta later. It is exactly why `MachinePanel.serve` used to end with a hand-written `mirror(model)` call, and running the set once between `binder.accept(...)` and `session.open()` is what let that call be DELETED rather than renamed |
| **THE ROUND TRIP IS NOT OVERHEAD, IT IS THE DELIVERY — and the second axis is who owns the EFFECT, which is a different question** | Vanilla answers both and the pair is the whole taxonomy. A chest pauses before it opens because the server has to say what is inside; the inventory opens instantly because `thePlayer.inventory` is already replicated, and `Minecraft.runTick` calls `displayGuiScreen(new GuiInventory(...))` on the spot — the packet beside it is for the ACHIEVEMENT and nothing waits on it. So "should this be networked" is not about latency: a window whose contents live on the server cannot open sooner than they arrive, and opening early just shows an empty box. Measured here at **~58ms average in singleplayer** (two 0–50ms tick alignments, since both ends drain on tick START and sends are immediate), which is the identical budget every vanilla container pays. **Then the second axis**: the inventory is client-opened and every click is still `C0EPacketClickWindow`, so a client-only UI that sends its own messages is a first-class shape rather than a hedge. What it CANNOT inherit is validation — `Event.sanitize(W widget, P value)` takes the widget, so a server holding no tree has nothing to clamp against and must check by hand. That makes the rule two-part: client has the data AND the action is self-checkable (a coordinate, an id to re-resolve, an enum) → client-only plus `ProtocolConnection.notify`; the action's validity depends on what was ON SCREEN (a slider's range, whether a button was enabled, which rows existed) → networked, because only a server holding the tree can answer it |
| **A SENTINEL OVER A VALUE THE CALLER CHOOSES IS NOT A SENTINEL** | The rate gate marked a slot empty with `payload == null && at == 0`, which conflates three separate things and is wrong on the host the javadoc invites: a payload is legitimately null (a button press IS the message), and a clock legitimately reads zero, because `setClock` exists precisely so a host can hand over a tick counter. Both together and the report is dropped and never retried. The identical shape sat one field along in `sent`, where `0` implicitly meant "never sent" — so on a tick-counter clock every throttled control held its FIRST report for a full interval, once per session, on that host only. Both are `boolean` flags now (`held`, `sentOnce`). **Invisible under `System.currentTimeMillis`**, which is what every test and every running client used, so neither could be found by exercising the feature — the second one was caught only by a test that set the clock to 0 on purpose |
| **A COMMENT BETWEEN TWO RULES IS NOT THE NEXT RULE'S SELECTOR — and a sheet REFUSED is a window with no styling and no error** | `ScopedSheets.scope` took everything from one `}` to the next `{` as the selector and split it on commas, and a comment is exactly where prose lives: commas to split on, colons to mistake for a pseudo-class, the odd brace. `machine.css` is a third comments and was refused whole — *Unparseable selector fragment near '.' in 'that.'*, `that.` being a sentence ending — so the panel drew from the UA sheet alone and read as "you made it look basic". Every rewrite test scoped a comment-free snippet and checked the string, which is why nine of them were green. **The warning was in the client log from the first run and was read on the third**, the same shape as the shader compile error that sat in a harness log — a log is searched, not tailed. Comments are stripped before scoping now, and `ScopedSheetParseTest` drives the REAL sheet through a real parser and asserts the panel root gets its width **(RETIRED at 6.8 with `ScopedSheets.scope`: nothing textual reads a selector out of a sheet any more. The lesson outlives it — the warning sat in the client log from the first run and was read on the third, because a log is searched rather than tailed.)** |
| **A FRAME'S CONTENT SLOT IS NOT THE FILL IDIOM — an unsized window is sized by its CONTENT, and every networked window arrives unsized** | `window > .__content__ { height: 0; flex-grow: 1 }` is right for a frame that has a size and contributes exactly nothing to one that does not, so a frame nobody sized was caption + 0, clamped to `min-height: 48px`: a strip with a scrollbar. `CgUiWindowMount` never sizes a frame — the host knows nothing about what is inside — and the only `resizeTo` on that path is `applyPersistedGeometry`. So the machine window opened healthy **once per session**, on an old desktop record left by a previous quit, and collapsed on the second open once its close had dropped the record: reported as *"the first open is healthy, but if I close and reopen…"*. The slot's basis is `auto` now with `flex-shrink: 1`, so an unsized frame is caption + content (GTK's natural size; what every GuiContainer is) and a sized one compresses the slot exactly as before; `window { max-width: 100%; max-height: 100% }` keeps a tall panel scrolling inside a window that fits. The standing rule against shrink is about CONTENT being compressed below its own size — this slot is a viewport, and being compressed is its job. **A test asserting the slot's height against the content's is the one that fails against the bug (28 for 400)**; asserting the frame "has a size" passes, since 48 is a size |
| **A PRESS IN A WINDOW'S CONTENT HAS ALREADY DECIDED WHERE FOCUS GOES — activation restores the focus memory for a press on CHROME, never for one in content** | `emitMouseDown` blurs what was focused and focuses what was hit (the frame, when the press landed on nothing focusable), and that is the engine's rule everywhere: clicking bare background deselects. `Desktop.activate` then ran `restoreFocus` on every press, and the "frame itself does not count as focused-inside" exception — added so a caption press on a floating tool window would light its rail button — made it consult the memory and hand focus straight back. Type in a field, click the panel beside it to leave: the field commits on the blur and then takes focus back, caret and ring and all, *unless* the click happened to land on another control, which is exactly how it was reported. The memory exists so dragging a window by its title bar does not lose the field you were typing in, and that is what it is kept for: the caption, the resize handles and the slot's own scrollbars restore; anything under the slot's public content does not (`WindowFrame.pressedInContent`). **`sendInputEvent` cannot see any of this** — it skips `emitMouseDown` — so the covering test presses at a POINT with `consumeMouseEvent`, and the caption-press test beside it is the counter-assertion. **And the memory must record the choice**: with the field still named as "where this window last had focus", the next caption press — to drag the window — restored it anyway. A content press that landed on nothing is recorded as the FRAME, so chrome restores that; it is recorded by the press listener and not the focus listener, because the focus listener must never record the frame (a caption press focuses it through the same walk, and that one must not forget the field) and only the press knows where it landed |
| **THE SERVICES ARE FOUR, THE RUNGS ARE A STACK, AND INERTNESS IS ONE PREDICATE ASKED BY TWO READERS** (M5 5.5) | `ui.service` splits the old 962-line `Input` into what it actually was: `Input` (platform sink, hit test, three-phase dispatch over the COMPOSED tree with per-listener retargeting, capture, activation, the cursor's `auto` rule, a `Chords` keymap seam), `Focus` (one owner, one traversal, focus navigation scopes, modality, `delegatesFocus`), `Animation` (timelines whose clock is the host's DELTA — so "the clock starts on the first tick" is structural — plus per-frame hooks OWNED by a node), and `Lifecycle` (freeze/thaw/destroy). **A live interaction is a `InputMode` pushed onto a stack**, so the drag→switcher→keyboard-move→modal ladder that was four hard-coded `if`s at the top of `consumeKeyboardEvent` is now push order, and `ModeStackTest` reads `Input`'s constant pool to prove the service names no gesture. **Propagation is the DOM's**: `stopPropagation` ends the walk, the same node's remaining listeners still run, `stopImmediatePropagation` ends those — the old `emitTarget` conflates them and stays beside the new `emitTargetDom` until M6. **A modified chord goes to the keymap BEFORE content unless the target `claimsChord`**, which inverts the yield lists a widget could forget an entry from. **Three defects the row-by-row tests found**: a modal blocks the scope CONTAINING it (a dialog is a scope itself, so `scopeOf(modal)` answered the dialog and nothing was ever blocked); a skipped box is not a CANDIDATE but its children still are (skipping wholesale put the modal itself out of reach — `hit-test` is the property that is subtree-wide); and opening a modal changes what is hittable with no pointer movement and no frame, so it must invalidate the hover itself. **Close watchers, light dismiss and the drag ghost are NOT ported** — all widget-layer, all M6 |
| **PROMOTION IS RECORDED ON THE NODE, NEVER WRITTEN ONTO A BOX — and a `TaffyTree.remove` MARKS NOTHING DIRTY** (M6.0) | Two findings from the same afternoon, both silent. **(1)** A `Box` is destroyed and rebuilt whenever its subtree is hidden, frozen or restructured, so `box.setHost(document.topLayer())` is lost on the next sync: a popup hidden and reshown comes back UNPROMOTED — clipped by its scroller again — and only ever after having been closed once, which is the shape that reads as intermittent. `UIDocument.promote`/`demote` record it on the node and the box tree re-applies the set per sync. **The pass must own BOTH directions**: applying promotions alone leaves a demoted box hosted where the LAST sync put it, so `demote` appears to do nothing, again only for something that had been promoted before. **(2)** `TaffyTree.remove` takes a node out of its parent's child list and marks **nothing** dirty (unlike `setChildren`, which marks the parent) — and because it has already updated the list, `sameChildren` then skips the `setChildren` that would have. So a subtree that goes away leaves its former siblings exactly where they were until something unrelated dirties layout: a removed row leaves a gap, a hidden panel keeps its space, and both correct themselves the next time anything else moves. True of any `remove()` since 5.3 and unreachable until something removed a node between two layouts; `BoxTree.destroy` dirties the host now |
| **A WIDGET'S TIER IS DECIDED BY WHAT IT COMPOSES, NOT BY WHAT IT IS** (M6.1) | `Dropdown` is a control by every reading of the word and it holds a `Menu`, so `widget/control` cannot have it: that tier may name only `control`/`text`/`scroll`, and `Menu` is `overlay`. Same for `ColorSelector` (composes a `Dropdown`) and `SearchField` (composes a `Tooltip`) — both moved to `widget/composite` (`widget/form` until the 6.10 audit, where the name was found to record the tier rather than the subject), which is above `overlay`. The ledger had all three in `control` on the obvious semantic reading, and `LayeringTest` is what says otherwise. **The tier is a fact about the dependency graph**, and the useful consequence is that it is checkable: *"a `Button` is general-purpose"* stops being an intention and becomes something a build fails over |
| **A SUBCLASS INHERITS ITS PARENT'S KIND UNLESS IT IS GIVEN ITS OWN — the constructor takes the `Name`** (M6.1) | `Dropdown extends Button` and `MenuItem extends Button` and `Menu extends Popover`, and each chains to the public parent constructor, which passes the PARENT's `NAME`. So all three reported the wrong tag and every rule they have in every sheet matched nothing. The old engine's `tagName()` was an exact-class lookup with a lowercased-class-name fallback and the same failure is recorded there for `ToolWindowFrame`: no background, no border, both titles drawn at once — *"it reads as the widget not having been BUILT rather than not being styled"*. The fix is a **protected `(Name, ...)` constructor** on any widget meant to be extended; the standing rule about WHICH answer is right is unchanged — a dropdown deliberately does not answer `button`, while a widget wanting everything the supertype has plus a modifier passes the supertype's `NAME` and adds a class |
| **A `::part()` RULE CANNOT REACH A NESTED WIDGET'S PART, AND 55 SHIPPED RULES DO** (M6.1) | `colorselector .__channel-row__ slider .__thumb__` styles a slider that a colour selector builds, through a part of the colour selector. Neither half survives the port: a part is a LEAF, so nothing descends from one, and the nested slider is inside the composite's shadow tree where an outer rule reaches nothing at all. Two mechanisms answer it and both are later work — a sheet SCOPED to the composite's shadow root (`StyleEngine.addStylesheet(sheet, root)`, which has existed since M5 5.2), or `exportparts`, which does not. Until then the rules keep working on the OLD engine, which still runs the game, and reach nothing on the new one. **Counted rather than converted**, with the number asserted, because the only failure available here is silent: nothing errors, the rules simply stop matching, and a composite comes out unstyled |
| **AN ORDINARY PER-FRAME HOOK RUNS BEFORE LAYOUT, SO ANYTHING THAT READS GEOMETRY NEEDS `Animation.afterLayout`** (M6.1) | The frame is `animation → style → layout`, which is the old engine's order too and its documented trap: *"a newly added element measures zero on the same frame"*. What needs the post-layout list is anything positioned FROM measured geometry — a popover and a tooltip flip and clamp against their own width and height, so placed before layout on their opening frame they are placed as if they were a point and jump on the next. The old engine spelled this as an `onLayoutChanged` override on the element; the new one has no such hook **by design**, because layout is one pass with no feedback into it. A post-layout hook may move a box and read a box and **may not add one** — a structural change would need another layout and there is no second pass |
| **`screenToLocal`'S ORIGIN MOVED, AND THAT IS THE FIX** — `UINode.toLocal` puts the box's own origin at zero (M6.1) | The old method converted out of surface pixels into the element's layout space and did NOT subtract the element's own origin, so its answer was an absolute layout coordinate comparable to `getRuntimeCache().getX()`. Genuinely useful, and the single most reliably-misread method in the old engine: read as "relative to the element" — as its own name says — it invites adding the origin back, which is what `snapZoneAt` did, so the snap zone a drag reported was displaced by however far along the desktop the window was and armed at roughly double speed. It never presents as a conversion, because it is wrong by a different amount every time. With the origin at zero the question disappears: a caller wanting the absolute coordinate adds `box().x()` deliberately, and one wanting the offset within the node — a caret index, a slider fraction, a drag delta, which is nearly everybody — needs nothing. **The paint hooks agree**: `BoxPainter` draws every box in its own space, so `paintContent` and `toLocal` are the same space and `TextField`'s `textOriginX` lost the `getX()` term that used to cancel out on both sides |
| **`uiScale` GOES ON THE BOX TREE'S ROOT TRANSFORM, NEVER ON THE PAINT POSE — `BoxTree.setRootTransform`** (M6.1) | The new engine's spelling of the standing rule that `getRootTransform()` is the ONE definition of what `uiScale` means. Layout composes every box's world matrix through the root transform, the painter takes its pose from that matrix, and hit-testing inverts the same one — so all three agree by construction. Push the scale onto the pose in the render method instead and the picture is perfect while every click lands at half the distance: reported as *"the mouse input Y is shifted down"*, which is what a 2x coordinate error looks like in a tall column, and the scroll wheel reaching nothing for the same reason. The parity scene does scale the pose by hand and is not wrong to — it takes no input, so nothing there can observe the disagreement, which is exactly why the first interactive scene on this engine is where it surfaced |
| **A BARE CLASS RULE HAS NO `::part()` TRANSLATION WITH A HOST, AND NEEDS NONE — `::part(x)` ALONE IS LEGAL AND MEANS "WHOEVER EXPOSES THIS"** (M6.1) | `.__v-scroller__` sizes the scrollbars and is deliberately keyed on a CLASS rather than a tag list, with a comment recording what the tag list cost: `ConfiguratorPanel extends ScrollerView` reported its own tag, matched none of them, and its bars laid out at zero width — invisible, while the wheel still scrolled perfectly. The port reproduced that exactly, from the other direction: the bars became shadow PARTS, which no outer class selector can reach. A **hostless** `::part(v-scroller)` is the faithful translation — it says the same "whoever exposes a part by this name", needs no list of subclasses to go stale, and this engine's selector engine accepts it (`::part(x)`, `*::part(x)` and `#host::part(x)` all match). Verified rather than assumed, because a `::part` rule that parses and matches nothing is indistinguishable from one that was never written |
| **A TOOLTIP CANNOT BE A CHILD OF ITS ANCHOR ANY MORE — a light child of a shadow-rooted node is NEVER COMPOSED** (M6.1) | `Tooltip.attach` parented the tooltip to the thing it describes, which the old engine wanted (an shadow part inherits the anchor's colour and font from the cascade for free). On this engine nearly every anchor worth a tooltip is a composite with a shadow root and no slot — a `Button`, a taskbar entry, a rail button — and a light child of such a node gets no box, no paint and no promotion, with nothing anywhere reporting a problem. It reads as the tooltip never firing. It goes in the DOCUMENT now, which was always where it ended up: it is promoted to the top layer the moment it shows, so its parent never positioned it. What is genuinely lost is the inherited colour and font, which `ua/overlays.css` states on `tooltip` outright — and that sheet was already fighting the same inheritance for `white-space`, on the argument that a tooltip is not part of its anchor's text flow. **And the join must be LAZY**: every widget that tooltips itself does so in its own constructor, where its anchor is in no document yet, so attaching only at attach time silently loses every one of them |
| **`worldX()` IS SURFACE PIXELS AND `left`/`top` ARE LOGICAL — subtracting the root's origin cancels its TRANSLATION and leaves its SCALE** (M6.1) | `AnchoredPlacement` read an anchor as `box.worldX() - root.worldX()`, which is right at `uiScale: 1` and wrong by exactly the scale everywhere else: every popup was placed at uiScale times its anchor's distance down the page, so a popover anchored halfway down landed off the bottom of the screen. The old engine records the same trap from the other side — *position a popup from the layout chain, never from `localToWorld`* — and the answer here is neither: convert **through the containing block's inverse** (`Transform2D.apply(root.worldToLocal(), …)`), which undoes the scale and every ancestor transform and scroll in one step and is the only reading that stays right at any `uiScale`. The world matrix is still what must be read, for the reason it always was: a layout rect knows nothing about scrolling, and every one of these popups hangs off something inside a scroller |
| **A WIDGET THAT PAINTS ITS OWN CONTENT MUST BE `Measurable`, OR IT MEASURES ZERO** (M6.1) | `TextField` draws its glyphs itself and has no child nodes, so nothing in the box tree had anything to size it from: it laid out **`215x0`** — present, styled, bordered, with its icon and placeholder drawing correctly and no height at all. A click aimed at it landed on whatever was BEHIND it, which in a `SearchField` is the search field, which takes no focus — so the box could not be clicked into and could not be typed in. **The old engine never needed this**: a Taffy leaf with no measure function fell back to whatever the sheet said, and the shipped sheets give every field a height. It is the widget a THEME sizes that breaks, and a theme that sizes nothing is the honest default. The height must be computed the same way the paint computes it, or the field crops its own text |
| **`toLocal`'s ORIGIN MOVED, SO EVERY "SHIFT IT INTO MY SPACE" HELPER IS NOW A DOUBLE SUBTRACTION** (M6.1) | `ColorSelector.withinX/withinY` subtracted the surface's own position, and its javadoc explains exactly why the OLD engine needed that: `screenToLocal` mapped into the space the element's box is expressed in <em>without</em> subtracting the element's own position. `toLocal` puts the box's origin at zero, so the shift now moves every point up and left by the surface's inset — a click on the colour square's bottom-right corner landing near its middle, **which is the symptom that javadoc records, running backwards**. A helper written to correct a coordinate system is the first thing to re-read when that system changes, and its own comment is the tell: it names the bug it was written for, and the port makes the bug the opposite one |
| **A CLASS-NAMED HOST NEEDS A `::part()` TWIN TOO, AND A SELECTOR SCAN THAT ONLY KNOWS TAGS WILL MISS IT** (M6.1) | `.__search-field__ .__field__` names the host by a CLASS the widget wears rather than by its tag, which is the right call there — the box's whole look is on that class so a caller can borrow it — and the twin generator only matched `tag .__x__`. Thirteen rules were skipped, including the one giving the field its height. **And the generator must be idempotent**: it appends a twin to the selector list while leaving the original in place, so a second run appended it again — `checkbox::part(mark)` three times over, which parses and matches identically and is pure noise. It dedupes on rebuild now, so re-running it is free |
| **A PSEUDO-CLASS AFTER `::part()` DESCRIBES THE PART, NOT THE HOST — and a STATE CLASS on a part is unreachable from outside** (M6.1) | The open state was a `__open__` class the widget flipped, which an outer sheet reads on a popover in the light tree and **cannot read on one inside a shadow tree**. A `Dropdown`'s menu therefore matched the base rule through `::part(menu)` — picking up the `opacity: 0` a closed popup carries — and matched nothing that lifted it again: it opened, promoted, placed itself correctly and drew at **zero alpha**, with every observable saying it was open and the screen saying nothing had happened. The web spells this `:popover-open` for exactly this reason, so `:open` is a real pseudo-class here now (`Styleable.isOpen`). **And the matcher had to be split**: `matchesOriginating` tested the WHOLE compound against the host, so `dropdown::part(menu):open` asked whether the DROPDOWN was open — a node that is never open. Parts before the pseudo-element describe the originator, parts after it describe the pseudo-element; that is CSS's rule and it was collapsed |
| **D1 REVERSED: A SHADOW TREE IS OPT-IN, AND THE SHEETS DECIDE — 21 of 44 widgets may not have one** (M6.1) | D1 asked how to classify a `__x__` NAME. Wrong unit: encapsulation belongs to a WIDGET — a node either has a shadow root or it does not, and every part follows — so the question is which widgets may have one, and the shipped sheets already answer it. **A widget may host a shadow tree only if nothing reaches THROUGH its structure**, because `::part()` has no spelling for a part under a part (`::part(a)::part(b)` is invalid CSS), a tag under a part (nothing descends from a leaf), or a nested widget's part. Measured by `tools/port/classify.py` over every sheet: **23 can, 21 cannot, 220 rules have no spelling at all** — `colorselector` alone has 51. **What a shadow tree buys is the SLOT**, not the part naming: a caller's content cannot land among a widget's parts, which is what `.__content__` cost three times over and why `ScrollerView`, `Menu` and `Button` genuinely need one. A widget that takes no content has nothing to protect and pays the whole cost. **And a subclass cannot un-shadow its parent** — `Dropdown extends Button`, so Button's decision is Dropdown's; decide the base class first. The batch paid this eleven times in sixteen widgets, every one silent and every one found by eye |
| **A COMPOSITE'S OWN STRUCTURE IS A PART; WHAT A CALLER PUTS IN IS CONTENT — and content needs a SLOT or no outer rule ever reaches it** (M6.1) | Three widgets got this wrong in the same way and each looked like a different bug. `ScrollerView` put a caller's rows beside its scrollbars, so they sized to the slot rather than the view. `Menu` appended its items into the `items` PART, which is inside its own shadow root — so every `menuitem` rule in every sheet was an outer rule reaching nothing, and a menu drew rows with no padding and no focus bar. `Dropdown` put its whole `Menu` in its shadow tree, which put the items inside a shadow tree TWICE over. The test is not whether the widget created the node: a `Menu`'s items are ordinary `MenuItem`s that a theme styles by tag, and a part is a leaf a rule addresses by name. **A `Button` therefore has a slot too**, last in its shadow tree — without one, anything appended to any button is in no composed tree at all: no box, no paint, no promotion, and nothing reporting a problem |
| **...and the SLOT must be told to fill, or the rows do not** (M6.1) | A slot is an ordinary box between the host and its content, so `align-items: stretch` on the host reaches the SLOT and stops there. The menu's focus bar is the row's own background, so rows that sized to their text left the highlight stopping short of the menu's edge — visibly not the full-width bar every native menu draws. `width: 100%` at DEFAULT origin on the slot, which is the same fix `ScrollerView`'s viewport needed for the same reason |
| **A SERVICE THAT IS BUILT IS NOT A SERVICE THAT IS WIRED — three complete mechanisms had no caller** (M6.1) | `Dismiss` was written at 6.0 with the whole spec algorithm (the popover stack, the invoker carve-out, the `shownBefore` counter, the close-watcher cascade) and `Input`, written at 5.5, invoked **none** of it: light dismiss and Escape were both finished and both unreachable, so a menu could be opened and closed by nothing. The detach path was the same shape from the other end — the old `unregisterElement` told five things that a node had gone (hover and press target, pointer capture, a live drag, the modal stack, the popover and close-watcher stacks) and the new tree told **nobody**. And `topWatcherIn` compared a watcher's enclosing scope against a bare `null` while `Focus.scopeOf` answers the DOCUMENT when nothing declares a scope, so every watcher outside a window frame was unreachable — which is all of them in an application with no desktop. **None of this is visible to a unit test of the service**, which passes whether or not anything calls it, nor to a widget test, which drives the widget's API rather than the platform's. They surface only by using the application, one screenshot at a time, which is exactly how they surfaced. `ServiceWiringTest` asserts each seam through the entry points a HOST calls — `consumeMouseEvent`, `consumeKeyboardEvent`, `remove()` — and a new seam gets a case there in the same commit |
| **...and the ordering M6.0 chose is the one that made this inevitable: the MACHINERY was declared done before a single widget had run on it** (M6.1) | Every defect of the sixteen-widget batch was an engine gap rather than a widget one — the defaults, hosting not implying out-of-flow, a missing `contentBoxWidth`, the three unwired services, a placement scale error, a tooltip attach point that assumed light children work on any node. The widgets were copied faithfully; what they landed on was unfinished, and each gap was found by eye. The lesson is not to port more carefully — the codemod was fine — it is that **a milestone which builds machinery must run something real on it before calling itself done**, and that `cgui-engine-parity` (one tree, both engines, two PNGs, diffed) was built at 5.4 and never extended to the widgets, which is the mechanical check every one of these would have failed |
| **HOSTING IS OUT-OF-FLOW, AND THE NEW ENGINE WAS NOT SAYING SO — a promoted popup was a flex ITEM of a zero-sized top layer** (M6.1) | The old engine forced `position: absolute` at IMPORTANT origin on promotion, and `ua/overlays.css` still records it in a comment. The new engine may not write into the cascade at all, so nothing replaced it: a promoted popover kept whatever `position` it cascaded to, arrived in the top layer as an ordinary flex item, and — with this engine's CSS-initial `flex-shrink: 1` where the old default was `0` — was **compressed to nothing**. Measured on a popover whose text child laid out at 56x52 inside a parent box of **60x4**, which then clipped its own content away: it was promoted, it had a box, the paint walk reached it, and on screen the popup did not open. Tooltip, Menu and Dropdown all went with it, for the one reason. `BoxStyle.apply(.., hosted)` decides it now, because being hosted somewhere other than where you sit in the tree is exactly what out-of-flow MEANS — a fact of the box tree rather than a style every popup has to remember. **And hosting is an input the computed style does not carry**: promoting a node changes no style of its own, so `refreshStyles` comparing `ComputedStyle` alone leaves a freshly promoted popup in flow |
| **`contentBoxWidth()` AND `contentWidth()` ARE DIFFERENT THINGS AND THE CODEMOD MAPPED ONE ONTO THE OTHER** (M6.1) | Taffy's `contentBoxWidth()` is the BOX minus its border and padding; `Box.contentWidth()` is the extent of what is INSIDE it. They are equal only by coincidence, and for a widget with **no child nodes** the second is zero. `TextField` draws its own text and has none, so `ctx.pushScissor(originX, 0, layout.contentWidth(), …)` was a **zero-width scissor** that clipped the field's text away entirely: the border drew, focus worked, typing worked, the model held the string, and the field was blank — which reads as the text not being stored, not as it being clipped. `Box` has both accessors now and the three widths are named in one place: `width()` the border box, `clientWidth()` the padding box (what scrolls), `contentBoxWidth()` the content box (where text goes). A codemod rule that pairs two similarly-named methods across engines is exactly the kind that must be read rather than trusted — this one was applied to three sites and wrong at all three |
| **A COMMENT BEFORE A RULE IS PART OF WHAT A SELECTOR SCAN READS, AND 94 RULES WERE SILENTLY SKIPPED BY IT** (M6.1) | The sheets' own house style puts a comment on the line above the rule it explains, so a scan splitting on `}` sees `/* 3px is half the bar height */ slider .__fill__` as one selector — which matches no pattern, so the rule is passed over. The generator adding `::part()` twins did exactly that: `slider .__spacer__` and `slider .__thumb__` got theirs, `slider .__fill__` did not, and the slider drew a bare grey rail with the thumb pinned at zero. **The identical bug is already an invariant row** — `ScopedSheets.scope` took everything from one `}` to the next `{` as the selector and refused `machine.css` whole. Strip comments to READ a selector and keep them in what you WRITE. It presents as the widget being unstyled, never as the tool having skipped it, because a tool that skipped a rule reports success |
| **D5.8 REVERSED: THE BOX TREE WRITES THE PROJECT'S DEFAULTS, NOT CSS'S — a default is the answer for every rule that does not mention it** (M6.1) | `BoxStyle` wrote CSS's initial for anything unset, on the reasoning that the old bridge's five divergences (`flex-direction: column`, `flex-shrink: 0`, `min-size: 0`, `align-content: flex-start`) are a standing source of surprise and that the sheets relying on them would be ported at M6. **The bill came due at M6.1 and could not be paid.** In a 6,200-line user-agent sheet nearly every rule leaves the direction unstated, so flipping it turns every unstated column into a row — and the failure is silent: `menu` states no direction, so its item column became a row, `align-items: stretch` stretched the items container across the menu's height, and a three-row menu drew **166px tall with its rows in the top 43**. Nothing errored; it just looked like bad CSS. The gallery scene met the same divergence three times in one sitting — a blank screen, a 42px-wide slot, an oversized menu — and each read as a different bug. Porting 6,200 lines to state a default explicitly is not a port, it is a rewrite with a silent failure mode. **Both engines answer the same question the same way now**, which is what makes a geometry difference between them a defect rather than a default; the divergences stay what this file documents them as — project decisions with reasons, not accidents to be corrected |
| **A SLOT IS AN ORDINARY BOX, SO IT SHRINKS TO ITS CONTENT UNLESS TOLD OTHERWISE** (M6.1) | `ScrollerView`'s viewport slot measured **42px inside a 776px scroller**, because this engine's `flex-shrink: 0` with an `auto` basis sizes a box to what is in it. A caller's `width: 100%` row then resolves against the slot rather than against the view, so every row came out the width of its longest word — which reads as the rows being unstyled rather than as the box around them having collapsed. The slot is `width: 100%` at DEFAULT origin now, and its HEIGHT is deliberately left unconstrained: content must be free to exceed the viewport, which is the whole point of a scroll container |
| **`box()` IS NULLABLE, EVERY PORTED GEOMETRY READ ASSUMED IT WAS NOT, AND THE FIX IS NOT AN ACCESSOR ON THE NODE** (M6.1) | A node has a box only while connected, unfrozen, and neither `hidden` nor `display: none` — where the old `getRuntimeCache()` always answered. So 185 unguarded chains came across in the port, each an NPE the first time the thing it measures is switched off: `ScrollerView.reserveCorner` reads a scrollbar's thickness and a bar that is not shown has nothing to read. Found on the gallery scene's first run, which is what that scene is for. **The convenience fix was written and reverted**: `UINode.boxWidth()`/`boxHeight()`/`boxX()`/`boxY()` answering 0 is `getRuntimeCache()` returning under a new name — it puts geometry accessors back on the node the three-tree design took them off, and it throws away the one thing the null was carrying, because **"zero-sized" and "never laid out" are different facts**. Guard at the call site, where the widget knows which of the two it is looking at and what the right answer is: zero thickness for a bar that is not there, `false` for a thumb that cannot be under the pointer, an early return for a host with nowhere to put a marker. And never zero for a DIVISOR — `Math.max(1f, …)`, because a fraction of zero is a NaN and a NaN poisons a whole layout with nothing having thrown |
| **A `::part` RULE IS INDEXED UNDER THE HOST, SO A HOST'S RE-MATCH MUST RE-MATCH ITS PARTS** (M6.1) | `checkbox:checked::part(mark)` is a rule about the mark whose every selectable input — the type, the classes, the state — belongs to the checkbox, so nothing about the mark itself ever changes and nothing marked it dirty: it kept the styles it matched under the host's PREVIOUS state, and the checkbox toggled with the mark still drawing unchecked. It presents as a widget being unstyled in exactly one state, which is the failure the old engine records three times over — once each for `:checked`, `:disabled` and `:hover`, once per widget that met it, and repaired every time by having the WIDGET flip a class of its own. That repair is what hid the general case: a class change does invalidate, so each widget that paid for it stopped being able to see the engine gap underneath. `invalidateStyleMatch` now marks the host's exposed parts too, and it is keyed on **every** reason a host re-matches rather than on state alone, because `.danger::part(label)` is the same rule with a class in it. **Only nodes carrying a `part` name are marked** — an unexposed node in a shadow tree is unreachable from outside by construction, so nothing about the host can have changed what matches it, and marking the whole subtree would do needless work on every hover of every composite. A nested shadow root ends the walk, since reaching into one needs `exportparts`, which does not exist yet |
| **A WIDGET'S STATE CHANGE HAD NOWHERE TO GO ON THE NEW ENGINE — the shadow boundary is the "does not travel" boundary** (M6.1) | `TreeObserver.stateChanged` has been on the seam since M0 and is honoured by `ServerTreeMirror`, and for the whole of M5 and the first widget of M6 **nothing ever called it**. A shadow subtree inherits a `null` observer, which is right for structure and for attributes — a part being inserted or gaining a class is genuinely not something a peer can act on — and wrong for state: `button.setText(...)` changes a node inside the shadow root, the report was dropped at the boundary, and a viewer kept whatever the description said forever. `UINode.notifyStateChanged` walks **out of every enclosing shadow tree** to the nearest node the far side has heard of, which is the old engine's walk out of shadow parts with the boundary spelled by the engine instead of by a flag. **The walk is a loop, not one hop**: shadow trees nest, so a widget built out of other widgets puts its parts inside THEIR shadow roots and stopping at the first host dirties a node that is itself invisible to a peer. Every observable was correct while it was broken — the widget, the server's tree, the flush's own return — the same silence as *"a dirty set that is cleared without being encoded is indistinguishable from one that was never filled"*, one step earlier: the set was never filled at all. **Assert on the ATTRIBUTION, never the count** — a test that only counted reports passes against a version dirtying the shadow label, which is the wrong node and travels to nobody |
| **A LAYER'S OWN REGISTRAR IS NOT ABOVE ITS LAYER — organisation is not a tier** (M6.3) | `LayeringTest`'s `LAYERS` is an ORDERED list, so entering `chrome/palette`, `chrome/menu`, `chrome/problems`, `chrome/notification`, `chrome/preferences` and `chrome/status` beside `chrome/` did not describe six neighbours, it declared six layers ABOVE the layer root — and `ChromeKinds`, the layer's `NodeKinds` service, must name every widget in the layer by construction. It came back as a layer reaching upward, naming ten things at once, which reads as the split having been wrong. It was not: `chrome/` is a PREFIX and covers everything under it, so sub-packages inside one layer need no entry at all. Ordering WITHIN a layer is a separate question with its own list (`WIDGET_TIERS`), and `widget` is the only layer that has ever needed one — because it is the only layer whose parts genuinely may not name each other |
| **`scrollExtent` IS ASKED OF THE MODEL, AND SHIPPED THREE MILESTONES BEFORE ANYTHING OVERRODE IT** (M6.3) | The node answers `-1`, meaning "ask the boxes", and 6.2 read that as a content-size accessor and got `-1` back for everything. `ListView` is what it was written for and is the engine's first override: a list realises a dozen rows of ten thousand, so the boxes under it describe the WINDOW and the children genuinely cannot be asked. It answers `size * rowHeight` on the axis it virtualises and `-1` on the axis it does not — the contract's own way of saying the children already know. **A test of it needs a model orders of magnitude larger than the realised set as its own counter-control**, because a fixture small enough to realise everything satisfies a content-derived extent perfectly, which is exactly the shape that hides the defect |
| **A PACKAGE SPLIT IS AUDITED BY WHAT A CLASS CALLS, NOT ONLY BY WHAT IT DECLARES — and Java has no sub-package visibility** (M6.4) | `widget.graph` was to become `widget.graph` + `.node`, and the obvious test — does this class declare any package-private members? — cleared `NodeWidgetFactory`, `NodeFieldBinder`, `NodeFieldWidgets` and `NodeCreationMenu`. `NodeWidgetFactory.placeholder` **calls** `GraphNode.bindToDocument`, which is package-private, so the move broke on the first compile. The deeper half is that `GraphNode`, `NodePort` and `PortDefaultEditor` cannot leave at all: they share `setSelected`, `bindToDocument`, `setConnectionCount` and the whole editor mount lifecycle with `GraphView` **by design**, and a sub-package is a different package to Java, so splitting them means publishing ten "only the view may call this" methods to every consumer. **The boundary a split can take is the one the encapsulation already has** — which is why `.node` is four files and not eight, and why the flat package was buying something rather than merely being untidy |
| **A `DataKey` IS INTERNED BY NAME AND ITS TYPE IS WHAT IT NAMES, so an engine and its COPY collide — and the OLD name is the one that must not move** (M6.4) | `DataKey.create` throws `already declared as ..., not ...` the moment both `com.crystalgui.widget.graph.GraphView` and `com.crystalgui.widget.graph.GraphView` initialise, which is any test that touches both — and during a strangler port that is most of them. The new copy takes the `.new` suffix 6.3 set for `menuBar.new`, never the old one: `ContextKeys.find` resolves a key **by name** out of a `when` expression, so renaming the shipped one silently breaks every command declaration that names it and nothing fails until somebody presses the key. It presents as an `ExceptionInInitializerError` in an unrelated test, because the collision is between two classes neither of which that test is about |
| **A VESTIGIAL SPI METHOD IS WORSE THAN A MISSING ONE, and the tell is that nothing overrides it** (M6.4) | `PortType.createInlineEditor()` returned "the control an unconnected input shows inline", was argued for at length in its javadoc ("only the type knows what editing one of these means"), and was overridden by **nothing in the repository** — so in production it answered `null` every time while `NodeFieldBinder.attach` supplied the real control through `NodePort.setDefaultEditor`. Worse than unused: `NodePort`'s javadoc for `onDefaultEditorChanged` records the bug the throwaway caused — a vector editor frozen at its first pre-layout 0×0 position, because `GraphView` snapshotted the generic control into a `PortDefaultEditor` one tick before the real one arrived, and that signal exists to work around it. Its one engine-facing return type was also the whole reason a port TYPE could not live beside the graph model it describes. **Check who overrides a hook before building machinery to preserve it**: the port's first answer was a `PortEditors` registry, and deleting the method instead made three files neutral with no replacement at all |
| **A BATCH'S DEFERRAL CASCADES, and the audit question is what a class names that is not SHIPPING — not what it names that is not LOCAL** (M6.4) | 6.4 was planned to defer two classes to 6.7 by scanning for imports pointing OUTSIDE the batch. `ShaderInspectorSections` imports nothing outside it and names `ShaderGraphEditor` four times, which is inside the batch and deferred — so it defers too, and the count was wrong until the compiler said so. The same shape will recur in every batch that holds anything back: a deferral is a property of the SHIPPING SET, and a scan keyed on directory boundaries cannot see it |
| **A LISTENER ON A SHADOW HOST CAN NEVER SEE ITS OWN PARTS — `event.getTarget()` is RETARGETED to the host before it runs** (M6.4) | The DOM's rule and this engine's: a listener outside a shadow tree sees the host, and a listener attached to the host IS outside its own shadow root — `UINode.retarget` walks up from the target and stops only at a root that is a shadow-including ancestor of the LISTENER, which the host's own shadow root never is. So the idiom the old engine used everywhere — one listener on the widget, an if-chain comparing `event.getTarget()` against its shadow parts — compiles, runs, and takes the wrong branch forever the moment those children become parts. `Scroller` fell through `thumb`/`head`/`tail`/`track` to `target == this`, whose branch jumps the thumb to the pointer: **a scrollbar that highlights under the cursor and refuses to move**, because a press ON the thumb jumps it to where it already is. Attach inside the shadow tree — a listener on the part is a shadow-including ancestor of itself, so nothing retargets — and `stopPropagation()` there, or the press bubbles to the track and the jump happens anyway. **A sweep finds this class mechanically**: any shadow-hosting widget that reads `getTarget()` at all |
| **A POPUP IS DRAWN ON THE FRAME IT OPENS, AND ON THAT FRAME IT HAS NEVER BEEN LAID OUT** (M6.4) | `AnchoredPlacement` flips and clamps against the popup's OWN width and height, so with no box it writes nothing at all and the popup is laid out at its containing block's origin — the corner of the top layer — and painted there. The old engine did not have this: `onLayoutChanged` fired INSIDE `calculateLayout()`'s `while (isLayoutDirty())` loop, so a placement write re-dirtied layout and settled in the same frame. **This engine lays out ONCE with no feedback into it and `Box` has no position setter**, so a post-layout hook's `left`/`top` write lands on the NEXT layout by construction — the plan's *"a post-layout hook may move a box"* is aspirational, not implemented. The fix is to lay an unplaced popup OFF-SCREEN on the same channel placement uses. **Opacity does not work and the reason generalises**: the sheets transition `opacity`, so writing 0 eases DOWN from 1 and the corner frame is still drawn at ~0.9 — anything used to hide a thing for exactly one frame must be a property nothing animates. `display: none` is worse: a popup with no box is one placement can never measure, so the state becomes permanent |
| **...AND A COMMENT EXPLAINING THE OLD COORDINATE SPACE IS A PORT HAZARD, NOT A GUIDE — the fix it documents becomes the bug** (M6.4) | `BlackboardPanel.dropIndexAt` carried a four-line comment stating, correctly and at length, that `screenToLocal` did NOT return a position relative to the row's own top-left, that "local" was therefore absolute logical space, and that comparing against a bare half-height had made every row measure as if it sat at the origin — so a pill always landed at the END of the list and could be dragged down but never back up. Every word was true of the old engine. **M6.1 moved that origin and the corrected comparison survived the port unchanged**, which inverts it exactly: `toLocal` now zeroes the row's own origin, so adding `box().y()` adds the row's offset within the list to the threshold and every row past the first reads as "the pointer is above my midpoint" — a pill lands at the TOP wherever it is dropped. The comment is what made the line look considered rather than stale, and it is why the sweep for `.x() - .x()` did not find it: there is no subtraction, only an addition on the far side of a comparison. **The general rule: a comment that explains a COORDINATE SPACE is describing an engine, and the port changes engines.** `InsertionMarker.indexFor` is the same walk written in the CONTAINER's space and is correct; either space works and mixing them is the defect |
| **AND A DRAG CALLBACK'S COORDINATES ARE RELATIVE TO THE SOURCE'S OWN ORIGIN — the other half of the same M6.1 change, and it reaches THREE consumers** (M6.4) | `Drag` converts the pointer with `toLocal(source, …)`, which since M6.1 puts the source's own origin at zero. The old engine's `screenToLocal` did NOT subtract the element's position, so a listener on a port received what was effectively an absolute layout coordinate — near enough the plane's space to be used as one, which is exactly what the graph did. So: the live wire's pointer end was drawn a whole node's width from the cursor; a wire dropped on empty canvas would have opened the create menu somewhere else again; and the marquee band subtracted the view's own offset from a coordinate already relative to that view, drawing the band far from the pointer **while selecting the right nodes** — because selection is computed from the raw coordinates and the band's `left`/`top` are written separately. **A test that asserts the SELECTION passes against the band bug**, which is what the first version of the covering test did. The DELTA is unaffected and most consumers use it, which is why only three of eight listeners were wrong: `Drag.toLocalVector` is a vector, and a vector has no origin to move |
| **`Box.x()` IS PARENT-RELATIVE WHERE `getRuntimeCache().getX()` WAS ABSOLUTE — so every ported subtraction of two boxes' offsets is comparing two different spaces** (M6.4) | The old accessor accumulated through every ancestor, so `a.getX() - b.getX()` was a legitimate way to ask "where is a, relative to b" for ANY pair. `Box.x()` is *"the offset from the host's border-box origin"*, so the same expression is only meaningful when the two boxes share a parent — and it is **silent** otherwise, wrong by an amount that depends on how deep in the tree they are. It killed the graph's wires: `NodePort.dotCenter()` returned a dot's few-pixel offset inside its own port row instead of its position on the plane, so every wire collapsed onto one short segment near the origin, drawn perfectly, in the wrong place, with the model entirely correct and the status line reporting the right count. **`Box.centreIn(box, space)` and `Box.originIn(box, space)` are the conversion**, through `space.worldToLocal()` — the only one that also carries the intervening transforms and scrolls, which a subtraction never did. Half-extents are added AFTER it, in the target's units: `worldToLocal` has undone every scale above `space` and a box's own width is unscaled, so adding them first scales them twice. **A sweep for `.x() - something.x()` finds the class mechanically**, and found five: the wire endpoints, the port editor's stub, the navigator's sidebar fit, `worldBoundsOf` (latent — the plane sits at the canvas's origin until a sheet gives it padding) and `visibleWorldRect`, which was wrong THE WHOLE TIME and is what culls both nodes and wires: it read the canvas's offset inside its OWN parent, so a canvas half way down a page reported a viewport hundreds of world units from the one it shows. **The viewport's own top-left is (0,0) in its own space** — the world point it shows is just the pan undone, and needs no box at all |
| **A COMMENT THAT EXPLAINS A COORDINATE SPACE IS A PORT HAZARD IN PROPORTION TO HOW GOOD IT IS — three of them in the compositor, each correct and each now inverted** (M6.6) | The M6.4 row above found one; the desktop had three, and they are the most carefully argued comments in the batch. `WindowMove`'s class javadoc, `snapZoneAt`'s method javadoc and `beginTearLoose` all recorded — at length, with the bug each was written for — that `screenToLocal` divided out the surface scale and did **not** subtract the element's own position, so a drag callback's coordinate was effectively ABSOLUTE and the work area's origin had to come off it. `snapZoneAt`'s own text says a previous version added the bar's origin back and "was therefore counting it twice", displacing the snap zone by however far along the desktop the window sat. Every word is true of the old engine. M6.1 moved the origin, so the arithmetic INVERTS: an offset along the caption needs no subtraction, and reaching the work area's space needs the caption's position ADDED — which reintroduces the documented bug from the other side. **The comment is what makes the line look considered rather than stale**, and a sweep for `.x() - .x()` finds none of these, because two of the three are additions on the far side of a comparison. Re-derive every coordinate site in a ported file from the engine it is landing on; do not translate one |
| **A PACKAGE SPLIT'S PRICE IS QUOTED PER PACKAGE AND PAID PER SET — two packages taken together cost far less than the sum of the two quotes** (M6.6) | `plan_m6.md` measured `.window` at **97** package-private call sites that would have to be published and `.motion` at **24**, and recommended taking neither: 97 on `WindowFrame` alone is publishing how `Desktop` drives its own windows. Taken TOGETHER, and with `.taskbar` and `.switcher`, the real bill was **52 members and 4 types** — because most of those 97 are pairs the split keeps on the same side. `WindowFrame`↔`SnapZones`, `WindowFrame`↔`WindowMove`, `WindowAnimator`↔`WindowAnimation` are each two classes with a shared surface, and a count of "sites crossing the boundary if THIS package moves alone" charges every one of them twice. **Measure the partition, never a package** — and the measurement is cheap: make the move, compile, and read the `is not public` errors, which is a two-minute answer where the static count took an afternoon and was wrong by a factor of two |
| **...AND A SPLIT'S PRICE CANNOT BE COUNTED FROM SOURCE AT ALL — three plan sections tried, and all three were wrong** (M6.5) | A regex cannot resolve a receiver, so `height`, `left`, `next`, `row` and `size` — package-private accessors on `TextEditor` AND ordinary locals in every one of its eighteen view parts — all counted as crossings. 6.5's static tool reported **285** members for a five-way split; the compiler reports **78**. Combined with the per-package error above, the two mistakes compound in opposite directions and neither is visible in the output. `tools/port/pricesplit.py` copies a package into a scratch tree, applies a candidate partition and leaves it for javac; `nested.py` runs first and is **not optional**, because a single unresolved nested type marks its class erroneous and javac stops attributing everything downstream — one `TextEditor.StableViewport` error hid **307** others and made the split measure as free, twice in one afternoon. **Read a zero only from a build that is genuinely clean**, and delete the static tool rather than keeping it for "exploration": a number that is confidently wrong is worse than none, which is this project's own doctrine applied to its own tooling |
| **THE PARTITION WORTH MEASURING FIRST IS THE ONE THAT KEEPS A HUB WITH WHATEVER IS WELDED TO IT** (M6.5) | The corollary that made the editor splittable after two sections had concluded it was not. `TextEditor`'s eighteen view parts reach 65 of its package-private members, so moving THEM out is 19 types and 65+ published members — and moving `TextEditor` out WITH them is **nine types and zero**. Everything else in that package reaches the editor through its public API, which is what a 194-member public surface is for. The old measurement asked "what if `.view` leaves", which is a question nobody was asking; the useful one is "what is this hub inseparable from, and what is merely near it". `AGENTS.md`'s own view-part entry had the answer already — *"a view part is a piece of the editor, not a client of it"* — and the error was reading it as a claim about the whole package when it is a claim about nineteen files |
| **A NULL `box()` REACHED FROM A PER-FRAME HOOK KILLS THE FRAME LOOP, AND A SCROLLBAR IS THE ONE THAT DOES IT** (M6.5) | The M6.1 row says `box()` is nullable and 185 ported chains assumed otherwise; this is where it bites hardest. `TextEditor.measureScrollbars` reads `verticalScroller().box().width()`, and **a bar that is not shown has no box at all** — so the very first frame of a fresh editor threw, out of the animation hook, out of `UIDocument.frame`, taking the whole loop with it. Fifteen sites in one batch, and the sweep has to be `box\(\)\.[a-zA-Z]` rather than a list of accessors: the first pass looked for `width|height|x|y` and missed `.padding()`, which `textOriginY` reads on every realised line. **Zero is the right fallback for nearly all of them** — a bar that is not there covers nothing — but never for a DIVISOR, where a NaN poisons a whole layout silently |
| **...AND TWO OF THOSE FIFTEEN WERE `toLocal` SUBTRACTED TWICE** (M6.5) | `rememberPointer` and `offsetAt` each read `toLocal(x, y)` and then took `box().x()` off the answer, which was exactly right on the old engine — `screenToLocal` did not subtract the element's own position — and is a double subtraction here. Every click in the editor would have landed displaced by however far down the page it sits, which is `ColorSelector.withinX`'s bug in the widget that can least afford it. **The tell is that an ADDITION survives a port and a SUBTRACTION does not**: a sweep for `.x() - .x()` finds these two, and finds nothing at the sites where an origin now has to be added instead |
| **A SHARED HELPER IN NO BATCH IS INVISIBLE UNTIL THE COMPILER ASKS FOR IT** (M6.5) | `ui/text/SyntaxHighlighting` writes colour spans onto `UIText` nodes, so it is engine-specific and had to be copied with the editor — and it was in NO batch at all, because the ledger's scan only walked directories that had a destination and `ui/text` had none. Its two neighbours are genuinely neutral, which is exactly why the directory had no single answer and got skipped rather than argued about. The scan admits `ui/text` now and `BATCH_OVERRIDE` marks the other two `stays`: **a file the ledger omits is a file nobody notices is missing**, and recording something as deliberately-not-ported costs one line and is the only form of that statement anybody can check |
| **AN OVERRIDE CANNOT WEAKEN ACCESS, SO PUBLISHING ONE BASE METHOD PUBLISHES EVERY OVERRIDE OF IT** (M6.5) | Moving the editor's eighteen view parts into `.part` made `TextEditor` reach `EditorViewPart` across a boundary, so `render(int, int)` had to become public — and then all fourteen subclasses failed with *"attempting to assign weaker access privileges"*, which is not an access error and does not appear in an `is not public` count. **A split's bill is therefore bigger than the access errors say**, by however many overrides hang off each published method. Worth knowing before quoting a number: the parts' own quote was 17 types and ~58 members, and the true figure was ~72 once the protocol came with it |
| **A MOVED FILE WHOSE `package` DECLARATION DID NOT MOVE STILL COMPILES, AND THE IDE IS THE ONLY THING THAT SEES IT** (M6.5) | Gradle passes every source file to javac explicitly rather than by sourcepath, so a `.java` sitting in `texteditor/fold/` while declaring `package com.crystalgui.widget.texteditor;` resolves exactly as it did before the move: same package, same access, **BUILD SUCCESSFUL**. IntelliJ reads the DIRECTORY, so it showed the split correctly and lit `TextEditor` up in red against a build that was green. Both are right about different things, and the compiler's answer is the misleading one — it is reporting on a tree nobody has. The tell is that a move which costs *nothing* has not really happened: this one cost 25 published members the moment the declarations were corrected, and a stale import then hid 39 errors on top |
| **A `Box` OVERRIDE IS THE COMPOSITOR CHANNEL AND AN ANIMATION SLOT IS THE CASCADE — porting one to the other is not a rename, and half a port leaves the value in the cascade** (M6.6) | `WindowAnimation` was converted to write `Box.setTransform`/`setOpacity` per tick and its four `startAnimationSlot` calls were left standing, so the START value went into the cascade and never came out: after a cancel the box's override was correctly withdrawn and `computedStyle().get(OPACITY)` answered **0**, so every window opened once and was invisible for ever after. Nothing failed — `EngineBoundaryTest` scans for `importantPipeline`, not for the ANIMATION origin, which the old engine legitimately used. **The channels are not interchangeable and the difference is the point**: a slot is a candidate the cascade resolves and must be ENDED; an override is read by the box tree, dirties the transforms itself, and is withdrawn with a `null`. Grep the file for `AnimationSlot` after converting one, and assert the RESTING value after a cancel — asserting the animating value passes against a half-converted port |
| **...AND `transform-origin` GOES WITH THE TRANSFORM, which is why `Box.setTransformOrigin` exists** (M6.6) | The old engine wrote the origin as a fourth ANIMATION slot, for the reason the standing row gives: `transform-origin` is not interpolable, so an origin re-resolved partway through a gesture changes what the same transform MEANS — a maximise eased about the CENTRE having been computed about the CORNER, reported as "chopped and flickering". Dropping the slots without replacing that leaves the origin to the cascade, which re-resolves it on any restyle. It is a compositor override beside the other two, resolved against the box being animated and withdrawn with them, so an origin cannot outlive its animation |
| **A CONCRETE `UINode` SUBCLASS THAT DECLARES NO `NAME` INHERITS `crystalgui:element` AND MATCHES EVERY BARE `element` RULE** (M6.6) | `NodeKindsCoverageTest` reads an INHERITED static field on purpose, so a widget that forgets its kind is caught rather than silently answering the base tag — the trap M6.2 hit five times. It caught four here: `Desktop`, `Taskbar` and `WindowFrame` are named by the sheets outright, and `WindowSwitcher`, `WindowPreview` and `WindowThumbnail` are named by none, which is exactly why they were missed. **"No rule names this tag" is not a reason to skip it** — it is a reason the mistake is invisible. The counter-case is a class that is BUILT and never decoded (`Resizer`): registering one with a factory that throws fails the same walk, so it is registered with a real factory that makes an unbound instance |
