| **A COMPOSITOR CANNOT BE BUILT IN A FIXTURE'S DOCUMENT, because a desktop with no window has no tree** (M6.6) | `Desktop` takes up no space until a window is open — the engine's most-repeated rule — so an empty one has no work area, no strip and no entry, and a style fixture built that way agrees with any sheet at all. It has to open a window, which means running frames, which means it brings its own document; appending it to the test's afterwards MOVES it across documents mid-walk and tears the subtree down (`ScrollerView.document()` was null inside `connected()`). `StyleParityTest` honours a subject that arrives already connected. The same shape as every other fixture rule in that file: *an empty fixture passes by having no tree to disagree with* |
| **`hidden` IS STRUCTURAL BECAUSE THIS SELECTOR ENGINE HAS NO ATTRIBUTE SELECTORS** (M6.0) | The obvious design — and `plan_m6.md` D5's — is HTML's `[hidden] { display: none }` in the user-agent sheet, one origin below an author so a theme can still restyle a hidden node. It cannot be written: attribute selectors are listed with `:nth-child` and the sibling combinators as deliberately absent, so the rule would parse into nothing and every `setDisplayed(false)` would silently do nothing. `BoxTree` gives a hidden node no box instead, exactly as it gives none to `display: none` — which is also what the old engine effectively did, since its `IMPORTANT`-origin `display` outranked every author sheet too. **And it must REPORT a structure change**: the box tree walks the composed tree only on frames the node tree said one happened, so an attribute that decides whether a box EXISTS has to say so, or the box outlives the hide until something else moves |
| **A KIND'S `Name` IS DECLARED ON THE CLASS IT NAMES, AND `UINodeRegistry.ENTRIES` MUST STAY ABOVE ITS STATIC BLOCK** (M5, corrected 2026-08-31) | `Name` held `ELEMENT`/`DOCUMENT`/`SHADOW_ROOT`/`SLOT` as constants, which reads as a vocabulary and is really a SECOND REGISTRY: two places to look up what a kind is called, and only one of them extensible from outside the module — a widget declares its own `NAME` beside its class, so four built-ins doing otherwise made the pattern a special case. They are `UINode.NAME`, `UIDocument.NAME`, `ShadowRoot.NAME` and `UISlot.NAME` now. **`Name.of(local)` is the default-namespace overload and is NOT `parse`**: it takes the LOCAL half, so a colon fails its character check, where `parse` is the reader meant to take either shape — if `of` ever split, a mod naming `mymod:machine` would land in `crystalgui` and collide with ours, which is the one thing a namespace exists to prevent. **The ordering trap is the expensive half**: the registry's static block now reads `UINode.NAME`, which initialises `UINode`, and the porting guide has every widget self-registering from its own initialiser — so the moment one of these classes gains one, initialisation RE-ENTERS `UINodeRegistry` while it is still initialising. The JVM lets a thread straight through its own in-progress init rather than deadlocking, so that is safe **only** while `ENTRIES` already exists; moving the field below the block turns every built-in registration into a `NullPointerException` on a class that plainly declares the map. And the built-ins stay registered BY the registry rather than by themselves, because `create` is the DECODE path: a class nothing has touched has not initialised, so a client decoding a description before constructing anything would find `element` unregistered. `shadow-root` is deliberately absent — never described, so it has a name for the cascade and nothing to build from the wire |
| **THE ENGINE'S TYPES WEAR THE `UI` PREFIX; THE GENERIC SEAM DOES NOT** (M5, 2026-08-31) | `UINode`, `UIDocument`, `UISlot`, `InputMode`, `UINodeRegistry`, `UINodeTreeSource`, `UINodeMirror` — `Node` was twenty classes across this workspace (`DockNode`, `GraphNode`, `ProblemNode`, `NodeMenuTree.Node`, Taffy's, tree-sitter's, Rhino's, JDT's `ASTNode`) and `Document` already means a FILE document here, which the workbench holds beside the UI tree root. **`NodeMirror<N,T>`, `TreeSource<N>`, `TreeObserver<N>` and `NodeContract` keep their bare names on purpose**: "node" there means a node of ANY tree, and `MirrorIsEngineAgnosticTest` implements them over a twelve-line class that has never heard of a widget — that test IS the assertion, so a blanket rename contradicts it. What takes the prefix is the IMPLEMENTATION over `ui.dom` (`UINodeMirror`, was `DomNodeMirror`), never the interface. **`EngineBoundaryTest`'s class lists are STRING LITERALS**, so no rename touches them: every renamed class silently fell out of `NEW_DOM_CLASSES`, was reclassified as old-engine, and the boundary scan failed naming its own subjects |
| **THE BOX PAINTER DRAWS EVERY BOX IN ITS OWN SPACE WITH THE POSE SET FROM `localToWorld` — the box model is the PAINTER's, and a skin only adds content** (M5 5.4) | `BoxPainter` walks paint order with each box's pose set to `base × localToWorld` — the same matrix hit-testing inverts, so there is nothing to reconcile and a mirror needs no `mirrored` flag: a thumbnail is a second box painted like any other. Background (fill+border under the children, as CSS stacks), the node's `paintContent`, children (scissored at the padding box for a square clip; the layer-FBO mask path for a rounded one), `paintDecoration`, `overlay`, `outline` — all resolved from `ComputedStyle`, drawables and compositing unchanged in `render/`. `TextNode` paints its retained paragraph at the content width layout settled on. The parity scene (`cgui-engine-parity`) builds ONE spec + ONE stylesheet on both engines and writes a PNG of each; `EngineParityTest` compares them and SKIPS with instructions when no GL run has produced them. **The harness's `crystalgui` branch does not compile against master's core** (`CgUiSlotScene` needs `widget.slot` from `native-content-slots`), so the scene was proven with an isolated `javac` against core's classes — running it waits on the submodule pointer |
| **A BOX IS LAID OUT ONCE, HIT-TESTED OFF THE MATRICES LAYOUT COMPOSED, AND HOSTED BY ONE FIELD — and the registry's initial for a margin is `auto`** (M5 5.3) | `ui.box` is the layout half of the new engine: one `TaffyTree` per `UIDocument`, synced from the COMPOSED tree only on frames the node tree REPORTED a structure change, restyled by `ComputedStyle` identity, computed once, read into `Box`es, and every `localToWorld` composed top-down from offset + host scroll + `transform` about `transform-origin`. `Box.hitTest` inverts exactly that matrix and walks `children()` (z ascending, insertion on ties) backwards, so a click lands on what will be drawn with no paint having happened — the old engine's four-place promotion is `Box.setHost`, and a thumbnail is `BoxTree.mirror`, a SECOND box with its own matrices rather than a subtree drawn twice against one cache. **Three things the first run taught, each silent**: (1) `MARGIN_*`, `PADDING_*` and `BORDER_*` carry `AUTO` as their registry initial — invisible under the old engine, whose listener fires only for a candidate so the bridge's `ZERO` stood, and fatal read through `ComputedStyle.get`, because an auto margin CENTRES an absolutely positioned box in its free space: `left: 200` laid out at 450, every popup somewhere plausible. `BoxStyle` states CSS's initial for every unset property and is the only place the two default lists (the bridge's five, the registry's) are reconciled. (2) The document's box is `display: block` unless a sheet says otherwise — a flex-row root stretches every child to the viewport's height. (3) Taffy asks a measure function for MIN-content as well as max-content, and answering both with one unbroken line pins a text leaf's minimum at its whole line; `Measurable.Fit` carries the question and `TextNode` wraps at 1px for the min. `OnePassLayoutTest` lays five trees out on both engines and asserts identical geometry — with every divergent default stated explicitly, so it compares arithmetic and not defaults |
| **THE CASCADE IS SHARED BETWEEN THE TWO ENGINES BEHIND `Styleable`, AND A SCOPED SHEET RANKS BY PROXIMITY** (M5 5.2) | `style/` is ~11,400 lines and nine files named `UINode` in 54 places; everything else was already host-agnostic, so the cascade is shared, not forked — a cascade bug is fixed once. `Styleable` is what those 54 places asked for: id/classes/type, a parent for combinators and a *different* one for inheritance (light vs composed: a rule outside a shadow tree cannot reach in, an inherited value can), nine state predicates, a shadow host and part name, the store, and three callbacks. `StyleEngine.addStylesheet(sheet, root)` is CSS `@scope`: only elements at or under the root can match, and `StyleSlot.proximity` ranks between specificity and order of appearance, so a composite's own sheet — scoped to its shadow root — beats an outer rule of equal specificity and loses to a more specific one. **The dirty set is drained parents first and until settled** (bounded), because a hash set matched a child before its parent and an `em` resolved against a font size that had not been computed yet. **The engine may not write at IMPORTANT** on the new host, and `EngineBoundaryTest` reads the constant pool for `StyleOrigin.IMPORTANT` and `importantPipeline` to say so |
| **THE NODE TREE REFUSES A MUTATION FROM INSIDE AN OBSERVER NOTIFICATION, AND RUNS LIFECYCLE AFTER THE MUTATION — never during** (`ui.dom`, M5 5.1) | Rows 36, 48, 78 and 281 are each the old engine paying for a widget building inside a callback that fired mid-mutation (*"an attach is not a moment to build things in"*). The new tree makes it structural: `UIDocument` queues `connected`/`disconnected`/`slotChanged` while a mutation is in progress and drains them once the outermost one has finished, slot assignment settled first; a callback may mutate freely, because it is a new mutation whose own callbacks join the same drain. What IS refused is a mutation from inside an **observer** notification — the mirror is being told about a change still being made, and a second change under it puts the edit script out of order. Thread affinity is asserted at every mutation entry through `UIDocument.require`, per document, so a headless tree is free and a painted one refuses everyone but its painter. **A shadow tree is invisible to the observer by construction**: shadow content is never a light child of anything, so a move across the boundary reaches the mirror as a `removed` or an `inserted` — what the light tree saw — never as a `moved` |
| **A CAPTION SHOWS WHATEVER THE TASKBAR SHOWS FOR ITS WINDOW — the monogram included — and a tool window shows nothing** | `WindowFrame.setIcon` withheld the monogram fallback on purpose (*"a caption that grew one would put a filled square on every dialog and tool window that has never declared an icon"*), and the reason was sound while every window that mattered declared one. A server's window declares nothing, so the commonest window of all had a red "M" in the strip and nothing beside its title. The rule is now a derivation rather than a choice: the caption draws what `WindowIcon.show` draws for the strip, and hides only when there is no entry to agree with (`isToolWindow()`), which is what the old rule was protecting. **And the server-side half of the caption was unwired**: `CgUiWindowMount.Mounted` inherited `MountedWindow.viewCommand`'s no-op default, so M4's `setIcon`, `setTitle` and `notifyUser` were shipped from `ServerScope` and dropped on arrival — every one of them looking wired from the panel's end. A default that means "this host has no surface for it" is the right default and the wrong thing to inherit silently on the one host that does |
| **A WINDOW'S CONTENT SLOT IS A `ScrollerView` — smaller than its content, a window SCROLLS it, with a bar** | `overflow: hidden` on the slot always scrolled (it is a scroll container; `scrollIntoView` from inside a panel relies on it) and never drew a bar, so a panel that grew past the work-area cap — the machine window's engine section unfolding — was cut off with nothing to grab. The browser model this engine is shaped on: the viewport scrolls the document. `ScrollerView`'s bars are overlays and it takes no focus, so layout, click-focus and the frame's focus delegate are untouched for content that fits, and `overflow: auto` shows nothing then. **The bars are the slot's FIRST raw children**, so `frame.content().getChildren().get(0)` is a `Scroller` now — three fixtures cast it to the `Button` they had added and failed with `ClassCastException`; `describedChildren()` is the public-content view and is what "the first thing I put in" means |
| **A WINDOW NOBODY PLACED OPENS CENTRED, and the cascade counter resets when the window is ALONE** | `placeByCascade` stepped from (0,0) — Win32's `CW_USEDEFAULT` — which put a correctly placed window exactly where an UNPLACED one is drawn, so the two were indistinguishable on screen and "it opens in the corner" could not be told from "it was never placed". Every GuiContainer in Minecraft is centred; the game has already decided. And the counter only ever grew, so a window closed and reopened landed one caption-step from where it had been and drifted across the screen once per reopen — found by the reopen test, at 20px, before it shipped. It resets whenever the layer holds no other frame: alone on the desktop there is nothing to cascade from |
| **A SCOPING PREFIX IS A DESCENDANT COMBINATOR, AND THE ELEMENT CARRYING THE SCOPE IS NOT ITS OWN DESCENDANT** | Rewriting a server's sheet as `.scope S` confines it — and silently drops every rule aimed at the scoped element ITSELF, because the class sits on the panel root and `.scope .machine-panel` cannot match the element wearing `.scope`. The rule carrying `width` and `padding` stopped applying, so the window opened as a sliver in the corner with no styling and nothing failing anywhere. CSS has no "this element or below", so each selector must yield **two**: `.scope S` and the scope attached to S's FIRST compound (`S₁.scope S₂…`), the latter placed before any pseudo-class. The failure is invisible in a test that scopes a selector and checks the output string — it looks correct — and obvious the moment a real panel is rendered. **(RETIRED at 6.8: the rewrite is deleted and `StyleEngine.addStylesheet(sheet, root)` is native `@scope`, which means "this element or below" — the problem does not arise rather than being worked around. Kept because the shape recurs: any confinement written as a PREFIX has this bug, and the two-selector workaround is what a prefix costs.)** |
| **A VIEWER MUST NOT BE TOLD WHAT IT JUST TOLD THE SERVER** | The value came FROM that viewer, so the echo carries nothing it does not have — and by the time it lands the viewer has moved past it, so applying it drags the control BACKWARDS to a value one round trip old. Under a drag it is continuous: measured in a running client at ~110ms of lag, the knob was hauled back to 0.51, 0.58, 0.67 and 0.74 while the user held it at 0.79. Mid-drag the next mouse move corrected it, so it LOOKED fine; on release nothing corrects it and the remaining echoes play out in order as a visible walk from the drag's origin to its end. **Suppressed only while the state still MATCHES what that viewer sent** — a server that clamped, refused, or had something else move it has genuinely new information and the sender must hear it, or the control is left showing a value the server never accepted. Everyone else is told regardless. Note the fix belongs on the SERVER: having the client keep its own value authoritative until confirmed is the same symptom cured by breaking the mirror's invariant, and needs a timeout heuristic to escape. **Rate policy is the amplifier, not the cause** — reporting every frame kept the server one frame behind and the staleness invisible; throttling to 20/s widened it to something the eye catches |
| **A CONCRETE CLASS METHOD BEATS AN INTERFACE DEFAULT, SO A FRAMEWORK HOOK MAY NOT REUSE A NAME THE BASE CLASS ALREADY HAS** | `Networked.mayClose()` was first written as `requestClose()`, and `UINode.requestClose()` already existed as the close-watcher hook — with the **opposite** sense, `false` meaning *"I did not handle this"* rather than *"do not close me"*. A panel is `extends UINode implements Networked`, so every one that did not override it answered `UINode`'s `false`, `mayClose` read that as a veto, and `WindowFrame.requestClose` returned without closing: **the X silently did nothing**, with no error anywhere. Shipped, and found by opening the game. **Every test passed** because each test panel happened to override the hook — which is the general trap with a default method, since the fixture that exercises it is exactly the fixture that hides it. The covering test is therefore a panel that overrides NOTHING, and it asserts both questions side by side so the distinction cannot quietly collapse again. Check a new hook's name against `UINode`'s public surface before adding it; `mayClose` was free, and the pairing with `ClientWindowContext.mayClose` is a bonus rather than the reason |
| **A REQUEST THE FAR SIDE MAY REFUSE CANNOT BE A NOTIFICATION** | The Machine example asked for its window with `onNotify("machine/open", …)`, and its own comment said the quiet part: *"the window arriving IS the answer"*. True while it always succeeds, and **indistinguishable from a lost packet** the moment it does not — the player presses the key and nothing happens, forever, with nothing to look at and no way to tell a rule from a fault. `ui/requestOpen` is a REQUEST whose reply carries **success only**: the window itself still arrives through the ordinary open path, so there is exactly one code path for "a window appeared" no matter who asked. And a **timeout answers the caller the same as a refusal**, because from where it stands they are the same — no window; what separates them is a log line, which is where somebody debugging should look |
| **OPENABILITY IS A FACT ABOUT A DEPLOYMENT, NOT ABOUT A CLASS** — so it is declared at registration and never on the `UiType` | The same panel may be openable on a test server and not a live one, for some players and not others. On the type it becomes a property every consumer of that class inherits, and a mod wanting the other answer has to write a second class. Declared per server, the resolver is handed the **viewer** and can differ per player with no second mechanism. **The default is refusal**: a client able to open any registered type could open a panel over a block it is nowhere near, or one a mod only ever shows from its own code |
| **...and `args` FROM A CLIENT IS A CLAIM, so a resolver RE-DERIVES and never dereferences** | The Rule-of-2 posture the widget contracts already take for event payloads, one layer up: a position is a position to look up and check, an id is an id to resolve. A resolver that treats `args` as a reference is a client choosing which object the server hands it. `null` is the refusal — an ordinary answer rather than an exception, because a client asking for what it may not have is expected traffic, and a refusal has to be as cheap to write as a grant or the check gets skipped. A resolver that THROWS is also a refusal: it runs on the connection's tick, and letting it out would take the connection down over one player's request |
| **A CLIENT-INITIATED OPEN ALMOST ALWAYS MEANS A `GuiScreen`, AND ONE THAT PAUSES THE GAME CANNOT BE ANSWERED** | `doesGuiPauseGame()` returning true stops `MinecraftServer.tick`, so the integrated server never pumps the connection, the request is never answered, and it dies at its timeout — the window simply never opens and nothing is logged. **Invisible on a dedicated server**, which is the configuration nobody tests the wire in, so it presents as "it works in multiplayer but not single-player". The same deadlock the editor already paid for when Phase 4 moved the workspace to the server; the difference is that this one is reachable by any mod on its first client-opened window |
| **A HIDDEN VIEWER MAY MISS STATE AND MAY NEVER MISS STRUCTURE** | Per-viewer visibility looks symmetric and is not. A tree delta **renumbers both sides**, so withholding one from a viewer that is not watching leaves it addressing elements by numbers the server has moved on from — and every message after that lands somewhere plausible and incorrect, because an id is an int and they all still resolve to something. Structure therefore goes to every viewer regardless, which costs nothing real: structure changes are rare and state deltas are the per-tick traffic. State is the opposite — keyed by id, so skipping it costs a hidden viewer only freshness |
| **...and a viewer coming back needs the CURRENT state, which a delta cannot give it** | A delta only says what moved, so replaying nothing leaves a returning viewer showing whatever was true when it looked away: correct-looking and stale. It is re-described instead, which is the LATE-VIEWER path and right for the same reason — a live description carries the ids the server is already using, so nothing renumbers. **The description has to be rebuilt first**, and that is the part that is easy to miss: a reshape rebuilt it and a plain STATE change did not, so the returning viewer was handed the tree as it stood at `open()` and resynced to values from however long ago. It reads as a stale delta rather than a stale description |
| **WHAT A LEGAL GESTURE COULD HAVE PRODUCED IS SANITIZED; WHAT IT COULD NOT IS REFUSED** | The whole shape of server-side validation, and each half is wrong applied to the other. A forged out-of-range slider value is CLAMPED and delivered, because the clamped value is one a user could have made — so the handler runs and the model stays sane, where refusing would need a second channel to explain itself and make every handler defensive. A disabled element, an inert one or a kind nobody asked for is REFUSED, because no gesture produces those: the report came from a client that is wrong or a peer that is lying, and the safe reading is the same for both. **The sanitizing belongs to the WIDGET** — a slider's bounds and step, a field's maximum length, a dropdown's option count are known nowhere else, which is precisely why it cannot live in the session. `NaN` is the case worth naming: `Math.max(0, Math.min(1, NaN))` is `NaN` and a model's `==` guard is false against it, so the model is poisoned and every viewer's bars read `NaN` for good |
| **A REFUSAL COUNTER IS PER VIEWER, AND ITS THRESHOLD STOPS THE VIEWER RATHER THAN THE WINDOW** | One refusal is noise — a delta in flight when a control was disabled is ordinary and racy — and a hundred is a client that disagrees about the tree or is not being driven by a user. Counting separates them. Per viewer, or one bad peer closes a window for everyone watching it; and the threshold ends that viewer's participation only, because the window belongs to the others. Minecraft kicks on packet flood and Chromium's `ReportBadMessage` kills the sending renderer; neither takes the document down |
| **A RATE POLICY MAY DROP INTERMEDIATE VALUES AND MAY NEVER DROP THE LAST ONE** | Which is the whole difference between a debounce and data loss: a slider released between ticks would otherwise report a value it was merely passing through. So a held report is never discarded when a newer one lands, only replaced, and it is always eventually sent. **Driven by the CONNECTION's tick**, because a client session riding one does not own a tick of its own — a held value with nothing to flush it never leaves at all, and the failure is a control that works except when you stop moving it |
| **A HOOK NAMED FOR A STATE SAYS NOTHING ABOUT WHEN IT RUNS** — `bound()` became `bindWidgets()` | The salvageable half of D9's merge. Both hooks stay, because they genuinely run at different times, and what was actually wrong is that `bound()` named a condition the panel is IN rather than the job to do in it — so nothing in the name said "attach your widget listeners here, again, every time the tree is replaced", which is the one thing a reader has to know or their listeners end up on widgets that no longer exist. `bindWidgets()` says it, and reads as the counterpart to `client(io)` running once |
| **A REASON THAT MEANS DIFFERENT THINGS ON THE TWO SIDES IS WORSE THAN NO REASON** | `Networked.closed(String)` handed the server a `CloseReason` NAME and the client the wire's human-readable detail — the javadoc said so outright, as though it were a design. So one panel class, asked one question, saw `"NOT_VALID"` on one side and `"no longer valid"` on the other, and any teardown that branched on it worked on exactly one. It is `closed(CloseReason)` on both now, with the enum promoted OUT of `ServerWindow` (a client naming a server class to hear about its own teardown is backwards) and a machine-readable **code** on the wire beside the detail. **Both still travel**, because the two consumers want different halves and neither is wrong: a panel branches on the code, a host shows the detail — "the block was broken" is what a player should read and `SERVER` is not. `parse` answers `UNKNOWN` across a version gap rather than throwing, a window ending being a poor moment to fail |
| **A HOOK THAT RUNS ONCE OVER A TREE THAT IS REBUILT LEAVES ITS HANDLERS ON A CORPSE** — `bound()` and `client(io)` are one hook now | A re-describe builds **fresh panel instances** over the fresh tree, so anything registered by the previous instance is closed over a panel that is now detached: it still runs, it writes widgets nothing draws, and nothing anywhere reports a problem. `client(io)` ran on the first mount only, which made that the ordinary outcome rather than an edge — and `ClientWindows` carried a comment admitting the gap and pointing at `plan_ui_host.md` instead of closing it. **The first attempt at this declined the merge as "unsound in both directions"**, which is true of the mechanics (run once and listeners are stale; run again and the router refuses the second registration) and stops one step short: the status quo was already the first of those two failures. The obstacle was that registrations were treated as things that PERSIST when they need only be REPLACEABLE — the session routes each method once and dispatches through a swappable delegate, which is not a weakening of the router's duplicate rule, since that rule catches two different OWNERS colliding and a repeated qualified method within one session is by construction the same panel rebinding itself |
| **A SERVER→CLIENT BINDING SHOULD READ LIKE THE CLIENT→SERVER ONE, AND WHEN IT DOES NOT, PEOPLE STOP AT IT** | `io.on(power, Switch.TOGGLE, handler)` and `io.project(power, Switch.CHECKED, model::isRunning)` are one idea pointing two ways, and the first shipped version of the second did not look like it — `project(name, from, to)`, with the widget nowhere in the signature. It took a reader ten minutes to place, which is the whole review finding: an API that resembles an existing one and then differs costs more than an unfamiliar one. **Naming the widget first is not cosmetic** — it is what lets `autoProject` recognise what is already covered by IDENTITY, which deleted two invisible rules with it (name your explicit projections; declare them before the automatic pass), each of whose only symptom when broken is a widget written twice a tick by two projections that may disagree, the later one winning |
| **A LIST PROJECTION MAY SKIP AN UNCHANGED ROW ONLY WHEN THE MODEL HANDED OVER A DIFFERENT INSTANCE** | Given the same instance twice, a value comparison cannot separate "nothing changed" from "mutated in place": a record is immutable and a plain POJO is not, and `equals` on a mutable row object is usually identity — so it answers EQUAL for an item whose fields have just changed. Skipping there freezes the row silently, which is the failure the whole mechanism exists to remove. So a fresh-but-equal item is proof the model re-snapshotted and is skipped; the same instance is re-applied, landing on idempotent widget setters and therefore costing comparisons rather than traffic. The conservative direction is the one that fails LOUDLY (a little wasted work) instead of quietly (a frozen row) |
| **A LISTENER ON A MODEL OUTLIVES THE WINDOW THAT REGISTERED IT** | `bind` connected to a model `Property` and dropped the returned `Connection`. The model is the long-lived thing, so the chain runs model → listener → widget → whole window tree, retained for good after close. The ordinary shape of a listener leak, and the ordinary fix: keep the connection and disconnect it where the window ends. Worth pairing with `Projections.close()` — a projection holds the model, the widget and its last value, and a keyed one holds every realised row, none of which is a leak while the window is discarded whole, but all of which is only true rather than assumed once something says so |
| **A CONVENTION MAY NOT GUESS WHICH SLOT A WIDGET MEANS — the contract has to declare it** | `Slider` carries `MIN`, `MAX`, `STEP` and `VALUE`, all four of them floats, so neither "the first slot" nor type matching can choose — and declaration order deliberately puts the range FIRST, because a value applied before its range is clamped against the old one. A convention that guessed would therefore be wrong on the widget it is most useful for. `WidgetContract.primary()` is the declaration and `null` is a real answer: `Dialog`'s title, `SplitView`'s weights and `Popover`'s mode are not "what the widget is" the way a slider's value is, so those are REPORTED as unwireable rather than assumed |
| **...and the report of what a convention SKIPPED is the feature, not the decoration — but a report that lists everything is a wall nobody reads** | Both halves were learned in one sitting. Auto-wiring that silently misses a field is the frozen-widget failure above wearing a nicer hat, so the misses must be stated (*"live and inert look identical, so a capability that can be silently skipped must say it is on"*). Then the first version stated ALL of them: seventeen lines on `MachinePanel`, fifteen being buttons, which is the sort of output a reader learns to scroll past — taking the two lines that mattered with it. Split by whether there is anything to ACT on: a field the model has no accessor for was never a candidate and is COUNTED, while a field whose accessor exists and still could not be wired is NAMED with its reason. Seventeen lines became one |
| **...and a walk up a panel's superclasses must stop before `UINode`, which has widget-typed FIELDS OF ITS OWN** | `parent` and `popoverInvoker` are `UINode`'s own plumbing, so a reflection walk that runs to `Object` reports the element's internals as unwired UI. `UiType.collect` has always stopped at `Networked` levels for this reason and the first `AutoProjection` javadoc claimed running past them was *deliberate* — it was not, and the evidence was in the report the moment it was pointed at a real panel. The boundary is passed IN by the caller, because `ui.projection` cannot name `Networked` without inverting the dependency |
| **A REORDER IS A `move`, AND THE TREE COULD NOT SPELL ONE** — `addChildAt` THROWS on a same-parent move | `hasChild` raises *"Cannot add the same child twice"*, which is correct and stays: it has caught three real double-parenting bugs (`CrystalEditor`, `StatusBarView`, `DockGroup`), each of which would otherwise have become a silent reorder. But it left the wire's `move` op — carried since M2 — with nothing on the engine side able to produce one for a container reordering its own children. So a move is spelled as a move: `UINode.moveDescribedChildTo`, which reports `moved`. The target index is read AFTER the child is taken out of the list, which is the only reading under which "move it to position 2" means the same thing from either side. Without it a keyed list projection's reorder reaches the far side as destroy-and-rebuild, losing the row instance and everything in it |
| **A GENERIC SEAM NOTHING GENERIC IS WRITTEN AGAINST IS NOT A SEAM, AND EVERY TEST PASSES EITHER WAY** | `ui.dom` was built generic — `TreeSource<N>`, `TreeObserver<N>`, `NodeContract` — for one stated reason: *"the mirror is written once; the engine swap underneath it is a port of the seam's implementation, not of the mirror"*. The mirror was then written as `ServerUiSession implements TreeObserver<UINode>` holding a concrete `UINodeTreeSource`, and **nothing could tell**: there is only one tree to mirror, so a mirror welded to it behaves identically to one that is not. The bill arrives a milestone later as a rewrite of op recording, coalescing, id allocation and the integrity check — the exact logic whose three defects were each silent. The cause was a **missing half of the interface**: it declared identity's READING (`idOf`/`peekId`/`byId`) and not its LIFECYCLE (`allocate`/`release`/`assignAt`/`resetIds`), so the one thing a mirror does had nowhere to stand. The only assertion that can see any of this is one that drives the generic code over a **second** implementation — `MirrorIsEngineAgnosticTest` mirrors a twelve-line `UINode` class that has never heard of a widget — and it is worth writing on the day the seam is, not on the day the second engine lands |
| **A DIRTY SET THAT IS CLEARED WITHOUT BEING ENCODED IS INDISTINGUISHABLE FROM ONE THAT WAS NEVER FILLED** | `onIdentityDirty` fired, `ServerUiSession` collected the element into `dirtyIdentity`, and the flush **cleared that set and sent nothing**. So `setEnabled(false)` on a live window was correct on the server, absent on the client, and produced no error at any layer — the same hole swallowed every class change and every inline style write, for as long as identity deltas had existed. It survived because *every* observable was right: the server's tree, the observer, the dirty set, the flush's own return. **Encoding it is only half the fix** — the client had no branch to apply an `a`/`y` entry either, so a test asserting the server sent it passes against a client that drops it. Assert on the far side's ELEMENT (`clientChild(1).isEnabled()`), never on the traffic |
| **AN ID IS ALLOCATED AT FLUSH TIME, NOT AT INSERT TIME — so a guard asking "is this described?" is asking about the FUTURE** | Ids are handed out in the order ops are encoded, which is what lets one `insert` op name a whole subtree as a contiguous `base`+`count` rather than an id per node. The cost is that a subtree added and removed inside one tick has **no id at the moment it is removed**, so `removed()`'s "does this have an id? if not, the far side never heard of it" test answers no and returns — leaving the `insert` standing with no `remove` behind it. The client dutifully builds a subtree the server has already destroyed, and is never told to take it away: a row that outlives whatever briefly created it, with nothing thrown anywhere. **The coalescing scan must come FIRST**, and the id test after it |
| **A COUNT COMPUTED AT `open()` DESCRIBES THE WINDOW THAT OPENED, NOT THE ONE A LATE VIEWER IS JOINING** | `expectedElementCount` is the integrity check a joining client runs against what it rebuilt, and it was fixed at open — so a window that had since gained a row quoted a count one short of the description it was serving, and the newcomer **refused the whole window** rather than one row of it. Recomputed on every reshape. The mirror case is what the check is FOR and is now the only thing it catches: two sides that genuinely build a different number of described elements, from a registry or codec disagreement. It no longer sees shadow parts at all — those stopped being numbered, so a client whose widget constructor builds one more internal label than the server's is now harmless, which is what it always should have been |
| **A PRISTINE DESCRIPTION AND A LIVE ONE ANSWER DIFFERENT QUESTIONS, AND ONLY ONE OF THEM CAN BE CONTENT-ADDRESSED** | A pristine description carries no ids, so both sides derive them from one document-order walk — which is exactly what makes it hashable and shareable: two windows showing the same thing hash the same, so re-opening costs one small packet however large the tree. Once a window has been reshaped a walk no longer reproduces the numbering the existing viewers hold, so a viewer joining afterwards must be **told** the ids (`UINodeMirror.encodeLive`/`decodeLive`), or every id it derived names a different element and no message lands where it was meant to. A live description hashes to something no pristine one matches, which is correct rather than unfortunate — a reshaped window was never going to share another window's cache entry |
| **A DELTA THAT ARRIVES BEFORE THE TREE EXISTS USED TO BE DROPPED, AND THE LOSS IS PERMANENT** | `ui/openWindow` carries a hash, so unless the description is cached the client must *ask* for it — and nothing tells the server the far side is not ready, so it goes on flushing. Both delta handlers began `if (… \|\| root == null) return;`. Worse than a dropped frame: `Property.set` returns early on an unchanged value, so a widget the server has already written is **never marked dirty again**, and the client shows the description's value forever for exactly the fields that changed early. The first tick is when a window mirrors its model, so this is the common case rather than a corner. Deltas are now queued and replayed **in arrival order** (a state delta computed after a renumber must be applied after the tree delta that caused it) and drained *before* `onWindowOpened`, so a host mounting the tree sees the state already sent rather than one frame of catching up |
| **EVERY STATE SETTER MUST BE IDEMPOTENT, because "mirror the model each tick" is the shape every server-side panel is written in** | `MachinePanel.mirror`'s javadoc states the rule — an unchanged value writes no candidate and marks nothing dirty, so mirroring more often than necessary costs comparisons rather than traffic — and `ProgressBar.setFraction` was the one setter for which it was **false**: it called `notifyStateChanged()` unconditionally, plus `invalidateStyleMatch()` and an IMPORTANT-origin write. So a panel mirroring a bar every tick sent a `ui/stateDelta` **per tick, forever**, carrying a value nobody had moved, and re-ran selector matching on the element every frame. Hidden for as long as every panel gated its mirror behind a dirty flag of its own — which is what makes it a trap rather than a slip, because the flag is the thing the rule says you should not need. The first panel to mirror unconditionally, on exactly those documented grounds, turned a quiet window into constant traffic **and nothing failed**: every one of those deltas carried the correct value. Assert on TRAFFIC (`InMemoryTransport.sent()`), never on state, or the test passes against the bug. `Dropdown` was checked and is fine — `select` guards, and `addOption`/`clearOptions` genuinely mutate. And the guard needs a `applied` flag rather than a bare equality test, because `ProgressBar`'s CONSTRUCTOR calls `setFraction(-1f)` while the field already holds `-1f` |
| **APPLYING A STATE DELTA RUNS THE WIDGET'S ORDINARY SETTER, WHICH FIRES THE WIDGET'S ORDINARY SIGNAL — and that signal is exactly what the reported-event wiring is attached to** | So the server moving a slider made every client that received it tell the server the **user** had moved it: one `ui/event` per viewer, for a gesture nobody made. It survived because it is harmless *in the common case and only there* — the echo carries the value the server just sent, so the handler writes the model back to what it already holds and `Property.set` returns early. It stops being harmless the moment a handler COUNTS anything or records who did it, and with two viewers it is attributed to the **wrong player**. `ClientUiSession.shouldSuppress` is the narrow ancestor of the fix — it stops a delta landing on a focused text field and resetting the caret, the same loop noticed from the one place it was visible — and it stays, because it stops the VALUE arriving rather than the report leaving. **Nothing could see it until something made the server move a REPORTING widget on its own**: every earlier server-side write went to a `ProgressBar` or a `UIText` (which report nothing) or wrote back a value the client had just sent (so the setter no-ops), which is why a suite of 1400 tests and a worked example both missed it. The covering test needs two viewers and a positive control — a guard written as "never report" passes every assertion about the echo and makes every control in the application dead |
| **WHERE A SERVER'S WINDOW APPEARS IS A HINT ON THE OPEN, AND A HOST THAT CANNOT HONOUR IT MUST OPEN THE WINDOW ANYWAY** (7.1) | Only the server knows what a panel is FOR — a machine's controls are a document you work in, a live log is something you glance at — and a client cannot tell those apart from the tree. So `Presentation` travels on `ui/openWindow`; without it a mod wanting the distinction ships a client-side table mapping type ids to placements, which is the registration this stack exists to remove. **The word "hint" is doing all the work**: a mount that refused a placement it could not honour leaves a player who pressed a key with nothing at all, and "it opened somewhere else" is a far better failure than "it did not open". `Presentation.parse` therefore never throws and answers `WINDOW` for anything unrecognised, which is also the version-skew answer — refusing to parse a placement is refusing the window. **And a CLIENT may not name one**: the placement is declared beside the resolver (`openable(type, resolver, presentation)`), because where a panel belongs is the mod's statement about its own UI and a restore — which is a client-driven open — holds no memory of how the window was presented the first time |
| **...and a networked tab is restored by ASKING, which needs a MANIFEST beside the layout** (7.1) | A described tree is the server's and cannot be rebuilt from a layout file — a layout holds an identity and never content — so a restored tab shows a placeholder and asks through `ui/requestOpen` for its window back by key. The gap that makes it fail silently is one level down: a networked panel's `DockPanelDescriptor` is registered on first sight of a window of that type, so on the next launch nothing has registered one and `DockLayoutCodec.decode` drops the ref before anything could ask. The tab was saved perfectly and vanished. `WorkbenchSession`'s `networked` key is the record, read BEFORE the dock; a ref whose type is not in it is dropped at read, because inventing a descriptor puts an unlabelled, unplaceable tab on screen. **A refusal drops the tab** — the machine was broken, the block was mined — since a placeholder with no explanation reads as the editor being broken, and **the drop must not fire while something is live behind the ref**: a refusal answers the ASK, and by the time it lands the window may have arrived some other way |
| **A DOCKED TOOL WINDOW ASKED FOR BEFORE ITS REGION EXISTS WAS DROPPED, WHILE THE WINDOWED ONE BESIDE IT WAS REMEMBERED** (7.1) | `ToolWindowManager.showPanel`'s windowed branch records a `pendingWindowedShows` entry and replays it when the workbench joins a window — the documented "the answer has to be given twice" rule. The docked branch's `if (host == null) return false` recorded nothing, so a panel asked for before the regions were built simply never opened. Invisible for as long as everything that shows a tool window did so from a user gesture, long after the regions exist; reached the moment a **server** could ask for one. **And the replay hook was a one-shot** (`return false` from the deferred `Animation.every` in `connected()`), which covers the frame the workbench joins a window and nothing after it — so a pending show recorded later was never replayed either. It runs from the workbench's ordinary per-frame `tick` now, which costs one `isEmpty()` on every frame but the few that matter |
| **A DOCK PANEL NEED NOT BE ABOUT A PATH, AND THE ACTIVE-PANEL SIGNAL ASSUMED IT WAS** (7.1) | `Workbench.othersEditing` did `Resource.of(activeFilePath())` and `activeFilePath()` is null for a panel that is not a file — a `NullPointerException` out of `refreshPresence`, raised from `DockArea.announceActivePanel`, which runs **inside the click that activated the tab**. Unreachable until something put a non-file panel in the editor area: every tool window is in a region rather than a leaf, so a networked tab was the first. Its own javadoc already had the answer — null means *nothing has been said*, and nobody is editing a thing that is not a file. It presented as the window failing to mount at all, because `ClientWindows.present` catches a mount's exception and takes the window off screen |
| **SUPPRESSING A HIDDEN WINDOW'S TRAFFIC MUST GATE THE WHOLE FLUSH, NEVER ONLY THE SEND** | A tree delta **renumbers both sides**. Gate below `flushStructure` — which is the obvious spelling and reads as equivalent — and this peer renumbers while the other does not, so every state delta afterwards lands on the wrong element, silently, because an id is an int and every one of them still resolves to something. Gating above both means the tree is simply not re-described while nobody is looking and the client's numbering stays the one it was last told. `aWindowReshapedWhileHiddenComesBackWithItsNumberingIntact` is mutation-checked against exactly that move |
| **`session.on` REFUSES a duplicate `(element, kind)`, and used to replace it silently** | A bare `Map.put`: the second registration won and which lambda ran was decided by registration order — the failure `MessageRouter`'s own duplicate check exists to prevent one layer down. It matters most for composition, where it is a boundary rather than a typo: a parent wiring an element its child already wired has reached inside the child, and the two behaviours cannot both run. A parent that wants to KNOW rather than to intercept takes a plain Java callback from the child |
| **...and the register-after-open rule was broader than its own reason** | It refused everything after `open()`, justified by *"the set of reported events is part of the description the client has already been sent"* — true of an element the client HAS been sent, and a tree delta re-describes a subtree in full with its reported events, which `ClientUiSession.wireReportedEvents` already re-wires. The honest test is whether the element has ever been **numbered** (`getNetworkId() >= 0`). Without the relaxation nothing can be added to a live window at all: no fragment, no row, no lazily built page |
| **A RAW NUL BYTE in a `.java` file makes git treat the whole file as binary** | `AttachedSources` used one as a cache-key sentinel (`key.add("\0jdk-sources=" + ...)`), deliberately and for a good reason -- no filesystem permits one in a path. Written as a raw byte rather than an escape it costs the file its diff, its blame and every future review, and it is invisible in every editor so nothing says why. It survived because a binary file simply stops being read. Escapes cost nothing |
| **A `transform` list applies its RIGHTMOST function first, and a single function cannot tell you which way you read it** | `SvgTransform.parse` composed left to right for as long as it existed and every shipped icon carries at most one function, so nothing could disagree. The idiom that can is scaling about a point — `translate(36 32) scale(0.42) translate(-32 -32)`, which is what Illustrator and Inkscape write for every nested group — and read the wrong way it put the logo's gem at `(-3, -5)`: off the canvas, no error, an icon that simply lacked a part. `SvgTransformOrderTest` pins the spec's own equivalence (`translate(10,20) scale(2)` **is** `matrix(2 0 0 2 10 20)`); the tree composition in `SvgResolver` was always right, which is why one level of `<g transform>` worked and a chain did not |
| **Whether an icon needs a TILE or IS one is readable off the file — `SvgDocument.usesCurrentColor()` — and must not be declared beside the name** | A chrome mark is authored as `currentColor` so the cascade can colour it; a brand mark names every colour it uses and must never be tinted. `WindowIcon` puts a palette tile under the first and knocks the glyph out white; under the second that draws a square behind a square. So artwork wears `__branded__` instead of a tile class and paints edge to edge (`overlay-origin: border-box` — the context's padding is the inset a knocked-out glyph needs, not one artwork does). `crystalgui:logo` is the first, and a pack dropping in its own gets the right treatment from the file alone. The design brief for the mark is in the SVG's own header comment: filled polygons and gradients only, no strokes, no `currentColor`, one shard placed three times through `<g transform>` — which is what made the transform-order row above a shipped dependency rather than a latent one |
| **An arc's segment count is per QUARTER TURN, never per command** | `SvgPath.arc` gave every arc the same `steps` whatever its sweep, so at the 8-step tile tier a rounded corner and a whole circle were both eight segments — the corner smooth, the circle an octagon, and a 296° letterform a rough polygon beside perfectly round tile corners. Invisible in the shipped set because Feather rounds corners with quarter arcs and nothing drew a large one small. Normalised on the quarter with `round` rather than `ceil`, so a corner is byte-for-byte what it was and float error cannot add a segment |

---

# Global coding rules

## Port, don't reinvent

**Anything that has been solved thousands of times — text editing, cursor movement, click and drag
selection, undo coalescing, layout — is ported from a battle-tested source and fine-tuned for this
codebase. It is not derived from first principles.**

These behaviours are *conventions, not derivable answers*. Each is one line, each is invisible when
wrong, and each was learned by shipping to millions of users. Four from `text/cursor/` alone:

| Rule | What happens without it |
|---|---|
| Auto-close fires on an **allowlist** (`;:.,=}])> \n\t`), never a denylist | "suppress before a letter" still opens a pair before `$foo` and `#define` |
| A plain arrow collapses a selection to its **edge**, regardless of which way the gesture went | Left-then-right on a backwards selection walks the caret instead of collapsing |
| A partly-commented block **comments out**, it does not half-toggle | Selecting a block with one commented line inverts half of it |
| A backwards word-drag **unions with the anchor word** | Word-granularity drag eats into the word it started on and stops feeling like words |

### Licences are load-bearing here

| Source | Licence | What you may do |
|---|---|---|
| VS Code / Monaco, CodeMirror 6 | **MIT** | **Port the code.** Attribute in the class javadoc, naming the source file. |
| **Chromium** | **BSD-3-Clause** | **Port the code.** Attribute in the class javadoc and in `THIRD-PARTY.md`. `RateEstimator` is one. |
| **Zed**, **wget** | **GPL** | **Read for shape only.** Copying would impose GPL on this repository. `Rope`/`TextSummary` take `SumTree`'s *design*; the progress channel takes wget's *refresh the ETA about once a second" and not a line of its code. |

### Port the module boundaries too

`com.crystalgui.text.cursor` takes its boundaries from VS Code, though **not file-for-file** — the
mapping is worth stating because two of the five come from elsewhere in that tree:

| Ours | VS Code |
|---|---|
| `CursorColumns` | `common/core/cursorColumns.ts` |
| `MoveOperations` | `common/cursor/cursorMoveOperations.ts` |
| `TypeOperations` | `common/cursor/cursorTypeOperations.ts` |
| `LineOperations` | `contrib/linesOperations/browser/linesOperations.ts` — **not** `common/cursor/` |
| `MouseSelection` | `browser/controller/mouseHandler.ts` — **not** `common/cursor/` |
| `ColumnSelection` | `common/cursor/cursorColumnSelection.ts` |

Monaco's `common/cursor/` holds twelve files; the six we have no counterpart for are its orchestration
layer (`cursor.ts`, `cursorCollection.ts`, `oneCursor.ts`, `cursorContext.ts`, `cursorMoveCommands.ts`)
plus **one** remaining feature gap: `cursorAtomicMoveOperations.ts` — `editor.useTabStops` for *arrows*,
where a left or right arrow steps a whole indent unit through leading whitespace. Backspace already does
(`TypeOperations.backspaceFrom` counts visual columns), which is the half that was reported; the arrows
still move by one character.

> **Every column question in this package is a VISUAL one**, and that is the single most portable thing
> about it. A box selection computed from character offsets is not a box — two rows whose indentation
> differs in tabs have the same character column at different places, so the "rectangle" comes out as a
> ragged edge following the text. The same rule is why Backspace takes one tab rather than an
> indent's worth of characters, and why a paste is re-indented by columns rather than by string length.

> This is not tidiness. The same logic first went in as private methods on `TextEditor`, which reached
> **2556 lines, larger than the entire `com.crystalgui.text` package combined**, and could only be
> reached through a `UIDocument` with fonts, a style engine and an input handler. Extracting it exposed a
> real bug within minutes — deleting the *last* line left a blank line, because the last row has no
> trailing newline to take and must swallow the one before it instead. The widget test never caught it:
> it only ever deleted a middle line. **Porting the algorithms without the boundaries keeps the
> algorithms and throws away the testability that keeps them correct.**

## CrystalGraphics ownership boundary

**CrystalGraphics owns the rendering backend. CrystalGUI consumes it.**

- CrystalGUI may define renderer-facing abstractions and UI draw orchestration.
- Fonts, shaders, framebuffers, VAO/VBO, draw submission, GPU resource ownership, and modern GL
  pipeline capability belong in **CrystalGraphics**.
- Never write raw GL, raw `float[]` vertex packing, or a hand-rolled buffer in `core/`.

Because CrystalGraphics lives in this repo and is directly writable, if CrystalGUI needs a new backend
capability we **add it to CrystalGraphics** and integrate against the new API — we do not reimplement
the backend here.

> The full infrastructure ownership map (`CgStreamBuffer`, `CgStagingBuffer`, `CgVertexWriter`,
> `CgInstanceWriter`, `CgBufferWriter`, `CgShaderBuffer`, `CgMesh`, `CgVertexArray`,
> `CgAbstractShaderProgram`, and the decision tree for picking between them) lives in
> **`CrystalGraphics/AGENTS.md`**, which `CLAUDE.md` already loads every session. Read it there;
> it is not duplicated here.

## Platform-agnostic core

`core/` must stay fully cross-platform — the import guard enforces no `net.minecraft.*`,
`cpw.mods.fml.*`, `net.minecraftforge.*`, `org.lwjgl.*`.

**The platform seam is CrystalGraphics'.** CrystalGUI has no registry of its own — it reads everything
through `CgPlatform`, and a loader registers exactly one `CgPlatformService` bundle:

| Need | Reached via | Lives in |
|---|---|---|
| Key/mouse codes, modifier state, **and the clipboard** | `CgPlatform.input()` | `platform/service/CgInputService` |
| UI sounds | `CgPlatform.sound()` | `platform/service/CgSoundService` |
| Presenting a cursor | `CgPlatform.cursor()` | `platform/service/CgCursorService` |
| Raw event sink (`Input` implements it) | — | `platform/input/CgSystemInput` |
| Code constants, cursor enum, cursor artwork | — | `platform/input/CgKeyCodes`, `CgMouseCodes`, `CgModifiers`, `CgCursor`, `CgCursorBitmaps` |

> **The clipboard is on `CgInputService`, not a service of its own.** It is not conceptually input, but it
> is reached the same way and needed by exactly the code that handles keys — two methods do not earn a
> registration slot. Both default to a no-op pair.

**No method in this SPI has a default, and neither sound nor cursor ships a `NOOP` constant.** A default is
an answer chosen for someone who never saw the question: a new platform compiles cleanly while silently
inheriting "no sound, no cursor, no clipboard", and inheriting a no-op is indistinguishable from deciding
on one. Abstract methods make the compiler the reminder — and a platform with nothing to offer still says
so, with an empty body in its own source.

> **Why this stopped being CrystalGUI's own registry.** `CrystalGuiCore` used to hold four static fields
> with setters. CrystalGraphics is the parent project and is always present, so two registries meant a
> loader had to find both — and could wire up one, leaving a UI with a working GL backend and no keyboard.
> One bundle makes a platform either registered or not. `CrystalGuiCore` now holds only `LOGGER`.

## Lombok

Prioritize Lombok to eliminate handwritten accessor boilerplate. It generates Java 8-compatible
bytecode and is `compileOnly` — no runtime dependency.

| Annotation | Use when |
|---|---|
| `@Data` | Simple POJOs, all fields in equals/hashCode/toString |
| `@Getter` / `@Setter` | Selective access — apply at field level when only some fields need accessors |
| `@RequiredArgsConstructor` | Immutable classes — pairs with `@Getter` only |
| `@Builder` | 4+ constructor parameters, or many optional ones |
| `@Value` | Fully immutable data carriers |
| `@ToString` / `@EqualsAndHashCode` | When you need one without full `@Data` |
| `@Accessors(chain = true)` | Fluent setters — used widely here |

Do **not** use `@Data` on classes with inheritance — use explicit annotations and always
`@EqualsAndHashCode(callSuper = true)` on subclasses.

# Package map

```
com.crystalgui.core            CrystalGuiCore — the global LOGGER, and nothing else. The platform
                               registry it used to hold now lives in CrystalGraphics; see below.
  .data                        CacheCell / IntCacheCell / LongCacheCell (dirty-flag memoization),
                               ReadOnlyVec2f (immutable view over a mutable JOML Vector2f), Transform2D
  .dispose                     Disposable, Disposable.Gl, Disposer — the ownership tree. NOT a
                               replacement for CgGraphicsLifecycle's registry sweep; it exists to
                               release on CLOSE rather than on exit, and to reach createOwned GL
                               objects no registry can see. docs/CGUI_WORKBENCH_SERVICES.md
  .property                    Property<T> (binding, equality-suppressing set), ObservableList<T>
  .signal                      Signal.Action/Value/Pair, SignalBase, Connection, ConnectionGroup
  .command                     Command (a named invocable action), CommandContext, CommandRegistry —
                               what a key binding, a menu item and the palette all point at. Plus the
                               MENU MODEL: MenuId (a named place a menu is drawn, interned, with nested
                               submenus), MenuSection (a group + its rows — what a separator is drawn
                               from), MenuEntry (Item/Submenu, sealed; an Item carries enabled/checkable/
                               checked so the RENDERER decides), MenuContributor (rows computed at open
                               time — the Window menu's editor list). CommandRegistry.sections() is the
                               one query every menu renderer reads; menu() is its deprecated flat view
  .undo                        Edit (one undoable change), CompositeEdit, UndoStack — one history per
                               DOCUMENT, never per window
  .window                      WindowState, WindowPolicy, DesktopPresentation — three types BOTH engines
                               name, so a package both may name. The D27 rule that gave 6.3
                               `core.collection` and 6.4 `graph.port`; a pure enum, a policy record and a
                               presentation enum, none of them naming an engine type at all. ScreenOverlay
                               was the fourth candidate and does NOT qualify: it holds a document and reads
                               its focus owner, so it is a facade OVER the engine rather than an SPI a host
                               implements, and it lives in `desktop.host`. plan_m6.md 6.6

com.crystalgui.widget.texteditor  THE EDITOR ON THE NEW ENGINE (M6.5) — TextEditor and
                               EditorCommands (its named actions). TWO files at the root; everything
                               else is in a sub-package below.
  .part                    VS Code's VIEW-PART decomposition: EditorViewPart (the base + Monaco's
                             shouldRender protocol), DecorationPool (the pool/hide idiom), and one

com.crystalgui.desktop         CRYSTALOS ON THE NEW ENGINE (M6.6) — Desktop (the compositor, found with
                               `Desktop.of(document)` and never built by a caller: the engine may not
                               name a compositor, so the compositor names the document), DesktopCommands,

  .window                      WindowFrame and everything a window IS: WindowChrome, WindowCommands,

  .motion                      WindowAnimator over WindowAnimation (transform + opacity, what a

com.crystalgraphics.platform   NOT CrystalGUI's code — CrystalGraphics' platform SPI, which CrystalGUI

com.crystalgui.ui.dom          THE SEAM, and the NEW ENGINE'S NODE TREE beside it.
                               The seam, generic in the node type and shared by both engines:
                               TreeSource<N> (stable node identity, light-tree iteration, contractOf,
                               one observer), TreeObserver<N> (an EDIT SCRIPT: inserted-with-index,
                               removed, MOVED, attributeChanged, inlineStyleChanged, stateChanged),
                               NodeContract (what a KIND is — name, reported events, whether children
                               may be described), UINodeTreeSource (over today's UINode; deleted
                               at M6.9). Written so the mirror is authored ONCE.
                               The node tree (M5 5.1), which implements that seam natively:
                               UINode (the DOM's Node, abstract: identity, the light and composed

                               (attributes, classes, the shadow tree, Styleable, geometry, state,
```
